BoE and APF flows</td><td>...and APF flows</td><td>90.6</td><td>89.8</td><td>0.8</td><td>25.8</td><td>16.8</td></tr><tr><td>5</td><td>ex. BoE and investment</td><td>...removes public investment</td><td>83.3</td><td>81.8</td><td>1.5</td><td>50.2</td><td>41.2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>6 Net financial liabilities</td><td>Includes illiquid financial assets</td><td>80.6</td><td>78.7</td><td>1.9</td><td>62.0</td><td>53.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>7 Net Worth</td><td>Includs non-financial assets</td><td>66.0</td><td>63.9</td><td>2.0</td><td>66.8</td><td>57.8</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan calculations

Further lifting the constraints on public investment. Labour could attempt to remove the impact of public investment spending from its targeted debt measure, in the same way it intends to do so from the (currently non-binding) deficit target. This would produce a £40bn lift taking total measured headroom to £50bn (1.5% of GDP, see item 5). The relevant point here is not the headroom contained within these rules, but the unconstrained ability to lift public investment. In a similar vein, a shift to a balance sheet target that takes account of non-liquid government assets would also allow create space for investment spending. For example, items 6 and 7 in Table 4 take account of, respectively, financial assets (such as student loans) and all assets including non-financial assets (e.g. buildings and transport infrastructure). Investment spending would not produce a deterioration in these metrics provided it is associated with a measured rise in the value of government assets (e.g. a new hospital). The downside is that assets can have volatile valuations (see Figure 1, blue line) and are illiquid. If the government places greater emphasis on these measures, it would probably have to do so based on the more stable trajectory of OBR forecasts rather than realized values. It would also be advisable to implement a net worth objective in conjunction with a more conventional target for debt that is widely understood and trusted by financial markets. The latter would be the binding constraint. But a clear focus on investment and longer term growth would strengthen the argument for altering the definition used in the debt rule as described above.

Figure 1: Measure of UK public sector net worth and net debt % of GDP, \*Heavy dashing is latest 2024 forecast and light is 2023 and 2022
![](images/3d7cd9e42c18dc5c77abd2faefb66720a545f7aa60c77195104dee935ea3f02a.jpg)
Source: ONS, OBR, J.P. Morgan

The immediate implication of greater public sector spending would be a slower path of deficit reduction over the next five years. But if the government manages to convince the OBR that this spending will lift growth and boost expected tax receipts, the projected rise in borrowing and resulting reduction in headroom would be smaller. Investment spending is typically associated with high multipliers on growth. Any subtle rule changes would need to be introduced gradually and alongside evidence they would have a benefit to longer run growth. But Reeves believes that “public investment is one important lever available to governments, with the potential to crowd in private investment”. If Labour manages to build a consensus around this idea, we note it would reduce the risk of a potential adverse market reaction.

## RBA: Spinning plates

There are concerns the RBA’s new employment mandate weakens its commitment to price stability

• It’s possible that alternate measures of full employment can diverge from NAIRU

But this does not appear the case currently, making balancing the employment and inflation goals easier

• And, independent of the mandate, the flat Phillips curve increases the costs of a faster return to target

Following last year’s external review, the RBA has moved toward assessing full employment based on a suite of spare capacity indicators. In the February SoMP, the Bank states that its aim is to achieve the maximum level of employment consistent with low and stable inflation and so its measures of spare capacity are primarily based on the rates of unemployment and underutilisation consistent with stable inflation/ labor cost growth. Still, there are some fears that the new mandate places pressure on the RBA to achieve a full employment goal at the cost of the inflation target.

We don’t think the new mandate is changing the RBA’s strategy dramatically. But, in this note, we explore broader measures of full employment not explicitly linked to stabilising inflation. In our view, the unemployment rate which achieves full employment is very close to the current estimates of NAIRU. So, even if the RBA’s full employment assessment is broader than a strict NAIRU concept, this should not change the usual trade-off between the inflation and labor market goals. The Phillips curve appears flat too, which raises the costs of a faster return to target, and calls for policymakers to let inflation absorb more of the adjustment to past shocks regardless of the mandate.

Figure 1: Core inflation and unemployment pre-pandemic
![](images/90143aac6f6484293e3c65756087caf05ff0d9fe06310b228e105a495530fbb5.jpg)

## Same, same but different

Prior to the pandemic, inflation consistently undershot the target and the jobless rate declined (Figure 1), suggesting the unemployment rate was higher than the rate consistent with stable inflation (u\*). Much ink has been spilled on the diffi-

culties in identifying u\*. The task is particularly hard when the Phillips curve is flat, as the unemployment gap will have only a small effect on inflation, giving the impression that the prevailing rate of unemployment is close to equilibrium.

Difficulties in gauging the tightness of the labor market are not unique to the RBA. At the 2018 Jackson Hole Conference, Fed Chair Powell argued that anchored inflation expectations and a flatter Phillips curve meant, “inflation may no longer be the first or best indicator of a tight labor market.” This cycle, particular attention has been paid to the vacancy to unemployment ratio as a model-free measure of labor market tightness. For instance, following the May 2022 FOMC meeting Chair Powell described a vacancy to unemployment ratio of, “one-to-one” as, “a pretty good number.”

Figure 2: Equilibrium unemployment rate based on Beveridge curve %
![](images/c89fa7cc272a8d9b2f8034b043498c974e0fc282de61d699c161f68bbd0594d7.jpg)

Michaillat and Saez (2024) argue that both unemployment and vacancies induce output losses and so full employment occurs when the sum of unemployed individuals and vacancies is minimized. Using this definition, we can estimate the unemployment rate consistent with full employment, by computing the optimal trade-off between vacancies and unemployment implied by the Beveridge curve. Figure 2 shows this measure of the full-employment unemployment rate (which we will now label U\*), and Figure 3 plots the measure against the actual unemployment rate.

Figure 3: Unemployment rate and Beveridge curve U\*
![](images/8b0234e397fcc7696b513f20c40edf3392f4c4beadf4025c5785d08679828b5e.jpg)

Based on this notion of U\*, the labor market was always inefficiently slack prior to the pandemic, as the unemployment rate was too high relative to the one that delivered the most efficient trade-off between vacancies and unemployed. In 2022, the actual unemployment rate slipped below the equilibrium rate (Figure 4), as vacancies were too high relative to the number of unemployed. This meant that the labor market was inefficiently tight—too many resources were devoted to recruitment rather than production.

Figure 4: Beveridge curve implied unemployment gap since 2020
![](images/0528b8d5c962a60ee78fe8026a766f40c7644b8c54625dbf92908b795990c71f.jpg)

Currently, the unemployment rate is about in line with the full employment measure at 4% (Figure 4) and fairly close to policymarkers’ latest estimates of NAIRU (both the Treasury and the RBA put NAIRU at around 4.25-4.3%). This is unlike in 2007, when the jobless rate was about 1%-pt below the Bank’s estimate of the unemployment rate consistent with stable inflation, but 1%-pt above our Beveridge curve measure of full employment (Figure 3).

When broader measures of slack suggest the labor market is much less tight than the NAIRU estimate, we should be more worried that the Bank might prioritise the employment goal at the cost of price stability (perhaps, because it incorrectly takes the divergence as implying the NAIRU estimate is too high). In the current environment, when full employment measures are broadly consistent, whether the Bank assesses labor market outcomes relative to a traditional NAIRU concept or a broader measure of slack won’t matter for estimates of the unemployment gap.

## All about sacrifices

With different measures of full employment about equal, the RBA is faced with the usual trade-off between inflation and unemployment. A key consideration here is the slope of the Phillips curve, which defines how large a deviation in the unemployment/output gap is needed to bring about a given change in inflation.

Pinning down the relationship between inflation and labor market tightness is tough because estimates are confounded by movements in inflation expectations, NAIRU and central bank policy over time. One way we can try and control for these factors is using panel data from Australian states<sup>2</sup> . This approach controls for region-specific effects, and uses time fixed effects to control for both deviations in long-run inflation expectations and changes in the NAIRU that are common across states through time.

Figure 5: Relationship between inflation and unemployment Adjusted inflation rate, %oya.
![](images/a50fd0ef65fa71fbadd5906aa149ff46347593d04974da72171dfa0a318a5c2f.jpg)
Source: ABS, J.P. Morgan. Inflation rate is residual inflation after adjusting for other controls.

Figure 5 shows the relationship between inflation and unemployment after adjusting for the two way fixed effects and lagged inflation. Scaling the state level results to the national level implies that a 1%-pt increase in the unemployment rate in one year is associated with a fall in inflation of just over a tenth in the following year. This suggests the sacrifice ratio is high—a large rise in unemployment is needed to generate a given fall in inflation.

We’ve argued previously that the recent inflation episode largely reflected the effects of cost-push type shocks (with a less significant demand component). Regardless of the weight placed on employment in the central bank’s mandate, policymakers will prefer to allow a greater degree of pass-through from cost-push shocks to inflation when the Phillips curve is flat, since the loss in output to offset the inflation shock is high. This suggests that some of the RBA’s motivation for pursuing a gradual return to target is independent of the mandate, as a faster return is much more costly in unemployment terms.

## Perception meets reality

So far, we’ve argued that changes to the mandate have not made price stability subservient to the full employment goal: broader measures of full employment coincide with current estimates of NAIRU, and part of the elongated return to target reflects the high sacrifice ratio. But that’s all immaterial if agents believe that tweaks to the mandate have weakened the Bank’s commitment to the inflation target. In that case, inflation expectations will drift higher, and the Bank will be faced with an even starker trade-off between inflation and unemployment.

We can assess the degree to which inflation expectations remained anchored to the target band by modelling inflation expectations as a weighed combination of lags of headline inflation and the inflation target. Figure 6 compares the weight placed on the inflation target (where a weight equal to one represents perfectly anchored expectations) before and after the onset of the pandemic. For all measures of expectations bar business, the degree to which expectations are anchored to the inflation target has increased since 4Q19. Taken on face value, this suggests that agents do not believe that the RBA’s commitment to price stability has waned.

Figure 6: Degree of inflation expectation anchoring Degree of anchoring, 1 = perfectly anchored
![](images/d62e8b5fd61556323cde73bff2652aeb90ce3f8f1757fd93eea89033dc3d1d0b.jpg)
Source: ABS, Melbourne Institute, RBA, J.P. Morgan

Of course, part of the improvement in anchoring reflects the monetary policy response delivered thus far, and if the Bank wavers in its commitment in the future credibility might slide. But the RBA’s policy response has been less aggressive than peers for most of the cycle (e.g., commencing hiking later, stepping down from large hikes earlier), suggesting that agents are so far comfortable that relative RBA dovishness is not coming at the cost of the inflation target.

## United States

• June jobs up a solid 206,000 but unemployment rate ticks up to 4.1%

• Most May spending data came in a little light, suggesting modest 2Q GDP growth

• Next week’s CPI report could influence messaging coming out of July FOMC

But before that, Chair Powell expected to tell Congress next week that Fed can be patient

The latest batch of economic data support the case for the Fed to begin cutting rates sometime later this year. The spending data point to only modest GDP growth in 2Q, and there may be some downside risk to our current estimate of 2.0% annualized growth. This would be after an already soft 1.4% outturn in 1Q. The latest data on wage and price inflation have also come in tame. Moreover, the minutes from the June FOMC indicated that the Committee generally saw a benign inflation outlook. While we are sticking with our call for a first cut in November, the risk of a September cut is growing.

The most important news this week was the June jobs report. While the 206,000 increase in unemployment was close to expectations, most other details were a little on the soft side. Most notably, the unemployment rate increased a tick to a cycle-high 4.1% and has ticked up in each of the last three months. Average hourly earnings increased by an expected 0.3% and the year-ago increase cooled further to 3.9%. Estimates of the prior two months’ employment were revised down by a net 111,000, continuing a pattern of generally downward revisions.

Other labor market indicators this week were mixed. Job openings increased in May, bucking the downward trend in that series. Continuing unemployment benefit claims for the week ending June 22—the week after the employment report reference week—rose to the highest since late ’21, suggesting that the labor market is continuing to soften heading into July.

The May activity data reported this week generally came in light. Construction spending eased modestly, the trade deficit widened to its largest since late ’22, and capital goods orders and shipments slipped again. There were other indicators that activity growth in the goods-producing sector is modest. The ISM manufacturing survey slipped further below the breakeven line of 50 and manufacturing employment slipped 8,000 last month. Service industry activity may also be softening as well, as the June ISM service survey broke lower.

The highlight of next week’s economic calendar is the June CPI report on Thursday. We and consensus are expecting another 0.2% increase in the ex-food and energy core CPI. Two days prior to that report Chair Powell will present his semiannual monetary policy testimony before the Senate banking committee.

## Joblessness drifts higher

The 206,000 increase in nonfarm employment gained support from the government sector, where employment growth rebounded to 70,000 last month, driven by state and local governments. Private employment increased by only 136,000 in May; health care and social assistance accounted for 82,000 of those jobs (Figure 1). Temp help employment contracted by 49,000—historically this category was seen as a leading indicator, though temp help jobs have been declining since early ’22. Factory jobs slipped by 8,000. The third print of jobs has now been revised lower relative to the initial print in nine of the past 12 months and it is very likely that the benchmark revisions will lower those estimates further.

Figure 1: Health care and social assistance employment Monthly change, 000s, sa
![](images/f111ff090d024ef760227e6be307cac1087888928cec85b84dd04e4bb903e602.jpg)

The workweek for all workers held steady at 34.3 hours, although it slipped a tenth for production and nonsupervisory workers to 33.7 hours. The 1.6% annualized increase in hours worked last quarter—when growth is tracking at or under 2.0%—suggests another soft quarter for productivity growth. Average hourly earnings increased 0.3% last month for both the all-worker and production worker series. For the quarter earnings were up at a 3.5% rate, or 3.2% for production workers. In the past Chair Powell has mentioned somewhere around 3.5% wage growth as consistent with stable price inflation.

Turning to the household survey, outside of the increase in the unemployment rate, most of the other details weren’t so bad. To higher precision, the u-rate increased from 3.964% in May to 4.054% last month. The three-month moving average of 4.0% is now up 0.5%-pt from its cycle low. Much of the increase was accounted for by adult women, for which the rate increased from 3.4% to 3.7%. By education, there was a notable increase in those with bachelor’s degrees, from 2.1% to 2.4%. The labor force participation rate moved up a tick to

62.6%, retracing half of the prior month’s decline. The employment-to-population ratio held steady at 60.1%.

The household measure of employment increased by 116,000 last month, though by 322,000 in the series adjusted to match the establishment survey concept; either household measure of employment still looks quite weak on a year-ago basis. Unemployment was up by 162,000 last month, indicating the vacancy-to unemployment ratio will likely continue trending lower. There was a notable decline last month in the ranks of those working part-time for economic reasons, and because of this the U-6 broad measure of labor market underutilization was unchanged at 7.4% (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Unemployment rates
![](images/6f485df48f3c036ce9a09929b0d65d67a7d44abd92393d5ce42c5cdd461b35ab.jpg)

The May JOLTS report revealed an unexpected rise in job openings to 8,140K from a downward-revised 7,919K for April (8,059K previously). This reading interrupted what has been—and is likely to continue to be—a gradual further cooling trend in labor demand. Both the job openings and hiring rates ticked up a tenth in May; at 4.9%, the openings rate remains modestly above the 4.5% reading at the start of the pandemic, which also was a tight labor market. This level of openings implies the vacancy-to-unemployed ratio remained at 1.2 (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Vacancy-to-unemployment ratio
![](images/15f3ef7e012bdde0d80b90f33fa7fdc64c3f2e93b73df7d13d9de3c2877a0bf2.jpg)

Initial jobless claims drifted up to 238K for the week ending June 29, with a tiny revision higher to the prior week to 234K. Jobless claims appear to have settled into a somewhat higher range than earlier this year, although broadly comparable with levels seen for much of last summer. Continuing claims also moved up modestly for the week ending June 22, to 1.858mn, and now look to be running above the broad range in place since this time last year.

## 2Q activity looking less robust

Away from the labor market, the May data on construction, factory orders and shipments, and international trade were all a little light, and suggest perhaps a little downside risk to our call for 2.0% GDP growth this quarter. Construction spending slid 0.1% in May. While construction spending has been on a sustained upward trajectory since the pandemic recovery began, momentum in this series has flattened out in recent months. Both orders and shipments of core capital goods—a monthly proxy for capital spending—fell 0.6% in May. Finally, the nominal trade deficit widened to \$75.1bn in May from a minimally revised \$74.5bn in April. Exports fell 0.7% on the month while imports declined 0.3%, with goods down and services up strongly across both imports and exports.

There was some mixed news in the June business surveys, depending on whether one looked at the PMI or ISM. The ISM services survey experienced an eye-catching tumble last month, though that concerning signal wasn’t mirrored in the PMI (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Service sector new orders/business measures
![](images/d82be287e0961f3fd522fb73a232fd13fbec1f53113d170630874a984561cb3c.jpg)

In next week’s June CPI we expect only a modest 0.1% increase, with the headline likely held down by lower energy prices and another soft gain in food prices. Away from food and energy we look for a 0.2% increase in the core CPI. Stillstrong increases in shelter costs are expected to be partly offset by weaker goods pricing.

Payroll survey week</td></tr></table>

We think that initial claims for jobless benefits declined by 8,000 to 230,000 during the week ending July 6. Claims data can and have been noisy around holiday weeks, and the incoming report will encompass the July 4<sup>th</sup> holiday. The timing of the holiday may result in a downward move in weekly filings for the week ending July 6. Initial claims have been drifting up in recent weeks, and how the claims data evolve over the subsequent weeks (in the absence of holiday-induced noise) will be an important signal about the health of the labor market.

Continuing claims had been range-bound for much of the past year but have broken materially higher over the past month. Continuing claims now stand at their highest level since late 2021, when they were rapidly declining as the post-pandemic recovery took hold. Given the recent rise in initial claims, continuing claims may continue to rise in coming weeks.

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Thu CPI Jul 11 %m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td colspan="4"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="10">8:30am</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Total</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa)</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.1</td></tr><tr><td>Core</td><td>0.36</td><td>0.29</td><td>0.16</td><td>0.19</td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa)</td><td>3.8</td><td>3.6</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.4</td></tr><tr><td>Core services</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Core goods</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Food</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Energy</td><td>1.1</td><td>1.1</td><td>-2.0</td><td>-1.2</td></tr><tr><td>Housing</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Owners&#x27; eq.rent</td><td>0.44</td><td>0.42</td><td>0.43</td><td>0.40</td></tr><tr><td>Rent</td><td>0.41</td><td>0.35</td><td>0.39</td><td>0.36</td></tr><tr><td>Lodging away from home</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Apparel</td><td>0.7</td><td>1.2</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>New vehicles</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Used vehicles</td><td>-1.1</td><td>-1.4</td><td>0.6</td><td>-2.0</td></tr><tr><td>Public transportation</td><td>-1.0</td><td>0.7</td><td>-3.1</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Communication</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Medical care</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.2</td></tr></table>

We forecast that the consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.1% in June, with the year-ago inflation rate moving down to 3.1% — a pace last seen in January. We look for a 0.1% increase in food prices, in line with the relatively soft growth over the past several months, and a -1.2% decline in energy prices (led by a -4% monthly drop in gasoline prices). Away from food and energy, we look for the core CPI to have risen 0.19% in June. If realized, year-ago core CPI inflation would hold steady at 3.4% after declining by 0.2%-pts in each of the past two months.

Shelter is the largest part of the CPI, and we continue to expect only a gradual easing in the pace of price increases despite much softer readings in some measures of market prices for new rental contracts. Specifically, we look for a 0.40% rise in owners’ equivalent rent and a 0.36% rise in tenants’ rent in June, which are broadly similar to the pace of monthly increase in each series in recent months. Elsewhere within the shelter component, industry data point to a third consecutive month of modest decline in lodging prices after seasonally adjusting; we forecast that it declined -0.4% in June.

After an anticipated rebound in May, industry data are once again pointing to a sizable decline in used vehicle prices on the month of -2.0%. That would be the largest monthly decline since January, and would more than reverse the 0.6% pop in May. We also anticipate a modest firming in new vehicle prices after declining for five straight months and seven of the last eight; we look for a 0.2% rise in June. In other transportation-related news, we look for a modest further -0.3% decline in public transportation after this highly volatile series posted an outsized drop the month prior. One notable source of uncertainty is motor vehicle insurance, which declined

unexpectedly in May for the first time in two and a half years.

Communication prices are expected to easing in June after postage stamp prices pushed up this measure earlier in the year—although additional increases have been approved to take effect next month. We look for a slowing in medical price inflation after medical insurance prices took a step down in April — assuming other medical prices do not offset as they have for the past few months. Following several months of sizable gains, we look for apparel prices to tick down for the second straight month.

We estimate that the producer price index (PPI) inched up 0.1% in June, a modest rebound from a -0.2% print in May. This reading would raise the year-ago PPI inflation rate to a 14-month high of 2.4% if realized. A further decline in individual energy prices, led by a more than 4% fall in gasoline, should yield a -0.8% drop the energy PPI in June, extending the large -4.8% decline recorded in May. Meanwhile, we look for a flat food PPI reading in June after two prior months of declines.

Away from food and energy, we look for core PPI prices to have risen 0.2% in June. This reading would be more in line with the underlying pace of core producer prices after several months of volatile readings. As such, we expect the year-ago core PPI inflation rate to tick back up to 2.5% in June from 2.3% in May.

Index (nsa)</td><td>77.2</td><td>69.1</td><td>68.2</td><td>70.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Current conditions</td><td>79.0</td><td>69.6</td><td>65.9</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Expectations</td><td>76.0</td><td>68.8</td><td>69.6</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td></td><td>3.2</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td></td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td></td></tr></table>

We look for the preliminary July report for the University of Michigan consumer sentiment index to have risen 1.8-pts to 70.0, following a further move down in June after a large drop in May. If realized, this increase would still leave the

index below its level a year ago. Recent volatility has been sizable around an upward trend, and we think the gains in equity markets and recent good news about inflation will help to stabilize consumer sentiment, even if the national average for gasoline prices is up slightly (about six cents per gallon) in recent weeks. Political uncertainty is a potential wildcard for the preliminary July sentiment numbers.

July will be the final month of the Michigan survey’s fourmonth transition from phone to web-based surveys. The survey organizers have stated that the two methods have produced very highly correlated results, although a technical document notes the possibility of an downward bias of around 1.6 - 1.7 points on the consumer sentiment index during this transition period. Of note, the recent weakness has been more apparent in the current conditions index, which reportedly is more impacted by the methodological shift.

Also receiving attention in the July preliminary survey will be measures of household inflation expectations. Both one-year and five- to ten-year ahead inflation expectations were at 3.0% in the final June report. For the near-term measure, that is not too far above its pre-pandemic level. But that reading for the longer-term measure is closer to the high end of the post-pandemic average.

## S&P Global manufacturing PMI (US) (Jul 1)

Index, sa

ISM manufacturing survey (Jul 1)

<table><tr><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Overall index</td><td>49.2 48.7</td><td>48.5</td></tr><tr><td>Production</td><td>51.3 50.2</td><td>48.5</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>49.1 45.4</td><td>49.3</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>48.2 47.9</td><td>45.4</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>48.6 51.1</td><td>49.3</td></tr><tr><td>Supplier deliveries</td><td>48.9 48.9</td><td>49.8</td></tr><tr><td>Export orders</td><td>48.7 50.6</td><td>48.8</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>51.9 51.1</td><td>48.5</td></tr><tr><td>Prices</td><td>60.9 57.0</td><td>52.1</td></tr></table>

The headline manufacturing ISM and PMI measures continued to move in opposite directions in June. The composite ISM manufacturing index slipped slightly to 48.5 from 48.7 in May, matching our forecast but disappointing consensus expectations for an increase. Meanwhile, the headline manufacturing PMI rose 0.3-pts to 51.6 from May’s level to a

three-month high, and near the top of its range over the past two years. (It did ease a tick from 51.7 in the earlier June flash release.) Looking specifically at the output components, ISM production was down 1.7-pts to 48.5, putting it below the 50-threshold for the first time in four months. Although the PMI output index also moved down modestly to 52.1 for June, that remains a still-strong reading. Stepping back, the ISM continues to imply a manufacturing sector that is broadly treading water, whereas the firming of the PMI over recent months suggests a continued—if moderate—recovery in manufacturing output.

In the details of the manufacturing ISM report, employment also slipped back below 50 after a jump in May; falling 1.8-pts to 49.3. Meanwhile the PMI employment index firmed further to 53.5, reaching its highest level in nearly two years. The change in manufacturing employment in the payroll data have been chopping around something just above zero over the past six months. By contrast, both reports signaled some improving momentum through an increase in new orders. This ISM component rebounded nicely from a slump in May; the 3.9-pt rise to 49.3 puts it close to its April reading; new orders improved 0.7-pts to 51.0 in the PMI report. Also within the PMI, the orders-to-inventories ratio encouragingly moved back above one, but the future output index slid notably on the month: down 6-pts to 62.3.

Turning to the pricing components, the latest data point to some easing in what had been an upward trajectory over the past year or so. The ISM prices index moved down sizably for the second time in as many months; it now stands at a sixmonth low of 52.1 after peaking at 60.9 in April. For the manufacturing PMI report, both input and output prices eased modestly on the month.

## Construction spending

<table><tr><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Nominal</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Private</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Residential</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.9</td><td>907</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Nonresidential</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.3</td><td>00</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Public</td><td>14</td><td>1.1</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.0</td><td>00</td><td>0.5</td></tr></table>

US construction spending slid -0.1% in May, disappointing consensus expectations for a modest gain. While construction spending has been on a sustained upward trajectory since the pandemic recovery began, momentum in this series has flattened out in recent months. The details revealed a -0.3% decline in private construction but a 0.5% rise in public construction. Within private construction, all of the decline in May was attributable to single-family units: nominal spending in that sector fell -0.7% in May, whereas multi-family was flat and home improvement spending rose 0.3%. The perpetuation of high-for-long interest rate policy boosted the benchmark 30-year fixed mortgage rate back above 7% this spring, and demand for housing appears to have softened in response across a range of measures. As we noted in a recent Focus, we see a likely pullback in residential construction during 2H of this year.

The report also included historical revisions to the construction data back through 2017, including a previously-reported -0.1% decline for April revised up to a 0.3% gain. For 2Q we are now tracking lower real residential and non-residential structures investment, whereas government spending is tracking slightly higher. Overall, these shifts did not meaningfully impact our 2Q GDP tracking estimate, which is broadly aligned with our forecast for 2.0% q/q saar growth last quarter.

## JOLTS (Jul 2)

<table><tr><td>Thousands, sa</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Job openings</td><td>8355</td><td>8059</td><td>7919</td><td>7950 8140</td></tr><tr><td>Hires</td><td>5617</td><td>5640</td><td>5615</td><td>5756</td></tr><tr><td>Separations</td><td>5330</td><td>5372</td><td>5337</td><td>5422</td></tr><tr><td>Quits</td><td>3409</td><td>3507</td><td>3452</td><td>3459</td></tr><tr><td>Layoffs</td><td>1601</td><td>1515</td><td>1542</td><td>1654</td></tr></table>

The May JOLTS report revealed an unexpected rise in job openings to 8140K from a downward-revised 7919K for April (8059K previously). This reading interrupted what has been—and is likely to continue to be—a gradual further cooling trend in labor demand. Both the job openings and hiring rates ticked up a tenth in May; at 4.9%, the openings rate remains modestly above the 4.5% reading at the start of the pandemic, which also was a tight labor market. This level of openings implies the vacancy-to-unemployed ratio remained at 1.2. While that is a current cycle and post-pandemic low, it remains notably elevated by historical standards. As we have noted previously, openings remain concentrated in small businesses (under 50 employees)—but also large corporations (over 5000 employees). Job openings have largely returned to pre-pandemic levels for the remaining firms sorted by size.

Meanwhile, the separations and quits rates held steady in May. The quits rate has remained at 2.2% for seven straight months, suggesting at best modest downward pressure on wage growth. While this is actually slightly below its prepandemic reading, wage growth remains firmer than its prepandemic pace, and the lack of progress on quits could be sign that wage growth may not slow appreciably further in the near term. That said, wages currently appear to running somewhat stronger than what the level of the quits rate might otherwise suggest based on historical averages. Shifts in bargaining power and other labor market attributes create some scope for this relationship to vary over time.

The JOLTS report is a month behind the monthly employment report due this Friday, but viewed broadly the readings are consistent with our expectation for continued firmness in employment and wage growth in June.

The nominal US trade deficit widened less than expected in May, to -\$75.1bn from a minimally revised -\$74.5bn in April. Exports fell 0.7% on the month while imports declined 0.3%, with goods down and services up strongly across both imports and exports. The report suggests a slightly larger drag for net exports than the 1.1%-points that our prior quarter GDP forecast assumes, although trade data can be rather choppy from month to month. The weaker-than-expected import data for May also point to the possibility that consumer spending and capex demand may run a touch softer than expected in our 2Q forecast.

Meanwhile, the May factory orders data also came in slightly weaker than consensus expectations, but the core capital goods data — which is what matters most for GDP tracking — had already been released previously. Taken all together, we view the May activity data as posing some modest downside risk to the prior quarter tracking relative to our 2.0% forecast for 2Q GDP growth.

## S&P Global services PMI (US) (Jul 3)

<table><tr><td colspan="5">Index, sa</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jun 23</td><td>May 24</td><td>Flash Jun 24</td><td>Final Jun 24</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>54.4</td><td>54.8</td><td>55.1</td><td>55.0</td><td>55.3 52.3</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>52.2</td><td>49.4</td><td>51.4</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>ISM services survey (Jul 3) Sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Headline composite</td><td></td><td>49.4</td><td>53.8</td><td>53.0</td><td>48.8</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td></td><td>50.9</td><td>61.2</td><td></td><td>49.6</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td></td><td>52.2</td><td>54.1</td><td></td><td>47.3</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td></td><td>45.9</td><td>47.1</td><td></td><td>46.1</td></tr><tr><td>Prices</td><td></td><td>59.2</td><td>58.1</td><td></td><td>56.3</td></tr></table>

Business surveys in the US have been sending divergent messages about the economic outlook for a while now, with the

composite ISM survey broadly trending a bit lower since mid-2022 while the PMI has been improving modestly on balance over the same period. The June services surveys sharply exacerbated that dichotomy, with the ISM business activity index plunging 11.6-pts to 49.6 while the analogous PMI component rose 0.5-pts in June to 55.3. The ISM measure now stands at its lowest level since the recession in 2020 whereas the PMI measure reached a two-year high in June. These conflicting signals do not help to clarify a forecast in which the economy does appear to be cooling but some are concerned about a more substantive pullback. The ISM has tended to be a better predictor for US growth dynamics in the past, although the fit is not especially tight for either survey.

The composite services ISM report for June disappointed expectations for a slight cooling, and instead slumped 5-pts to 48.8. The biggest swing was in business activity, but new orders also tumbled 6.8-pts to 47.3, which is the second-lowest reading since the start of the current expansion. By contrast, new business activity in the PMI improved 1.6-pts to a 12-month high of 53.5. Similarly, the employment index in the services ISM drifted a point lower to 46.1—its secondlowest reading this expansion—whereas services employment PMI improved 2.9-pts to 52.3, also a 12-month high. Setting aside one-off weakness in April, the latter has stayed in expansionary territory while the former has been sliding further into contraction.

The one consistent message from both surveys has been a stabilization of the prices measures in recent months at levels similar to their pre-pandemic ones—and well below their peaks in 2021 and 2022. The various price indexes across the two surveys all eased modestly in June.

Nonfarm employment increased 206,000 last month, which was very close to expectations. Most other details, however, were a little on the soft side. Most notably, the unemployment rate increased a tick to a cycle-high 4.1% and has ticked up in

Michael Feroli (1-212) 834-5523 michael.e.feroli@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank NA Michael S Hanson (1-212) 622-8603 michael.s.hanson@jpmchase.com

Murat Tasci (1-212) 622-0288murat.tasci@jpmchase.com

Abiel Reinhart (1-212) 270 4058abiel.reinhart@jpmchase.com

each of the last three months. The jobless rate now stands 0.7%-point above its cycle low. Average hourly earnings increased by an expected 0.3% and the year-ago increase cooled further to 3.9%. The average workweek held steady at 34.3 hours. Estimates of the prior two months employment were revised down by a net 111,000, continuing a pattern of generally downward revisions. The gradual loosening up of a very tight labor market is consistent with the Fed’s immaculate disinflation narrative and should give the FOMC confidence to lower rates sometime in the second half. We still see a first cut in November, but the path to a September ease got a little wider now.

In the details of the establishment survey, government net hiring rebounded to 70,000 last month, driven by state and local governments. Private employment increased by only 136,000 in May; health care and social assistance accounted for 82,000 of those jobs. Temp help employment contracted by 49,000—historically this category was seen as a leading indicator, though temp help jobs have been declining since early ’22. Factory jobs slipped by 8,000. The third print of jobs has now been revised lower relative to the initial print in 9 of the past 12 months and it is very likely that the benchmark revisions will lower those estimates further. The all-worker workweek held steady at 34.3 hours though slipped a tenth for production and nonsupervisory workers to 33.7 hours. The 1.6% annualized increase in hours worked last quarter—when growth is tracking at or under 2.0%—suggests another soft quarter for productivity growth. Average hourly earnings increased 0.3% last month for both the all-worker and production worker series. For the quarter earnings were up at a 3.5% rate, or 3.2% for production workers. In the past Chair Powell has mentioned somewhere around 3.5% wage growth as consistent with stable price inflation.

Turning to the household survey, outside of the increase in the unemployment rate, most of the other details weren’t so bad. To higher precision, the u-rate increased from 3.964% in May to 4.054% last month. The three-month moving average of 4.0% is now up 0.5pp from its cycle low. Much of the increase was accounted for by adult women, for which the rate increased from 3.4% to 3.7%. By education, there was a notable increase in those with bachelor’s degrees, from 2.1% to 2.4%. The labor force participation rate moved up a tick to 62.6%, retracing half of the prior month’s decline. The employment-to-population ratio held steady at 60.1%. The household measure of employment increased by 116,000 last month, though by 322,000 in the series adjusted to match the establishment survey concept; either household measure of employment still looks quite weak on a year-ago basis. Unemployment was up by 162,000 last month, indicating the vacancy-to unemployment ratio will likely continue trending lower. There was a notable decline last month in the ranks of

05 July 2024

those working part-time for economic reasons, and because of this the U-6 broad measure of labor market underutilization was unchanged at 7.4%.

Source: BLS. Conference Board, J.P. Morgar

## Focus: The u-rate signal from the labor differential

The June unemployment rate stands at 4.1% (4.054% to three decimals)—still low by historical standards, but 0.7%-pt above its April 2023 nadir. One timely indicator of the underlying trend in the unemployment rate is the jobs differential from the Conference Board’s monthly consumer confidence survey. It measures the gap between respondents who say jobs are “plentiful” versus “hard to find.” The larger that gap, the more favorable views about labor market conditions, and, empirically, the lower the unemployment rate on average (Figure 1). The labor differential has eased from its all-time high in 2022, but still remains near the top of its historical range and is broadly consistent with an unemployment rate running at or just above 4%. However, a modest deterioration in the labor differential could imply a more troubling rise in the unemployment rate: a move down to 15, which is still elevated by historical standards, would signal a further 0.5%-pt unemployment rate increase to 4.6%.

Figure 1: Unemployment rate and Conference Board labor differential %, sa Net %, sa; inverted
![](images/9dbd74b017dcb2bfefe7393ca0460745157b109b6b42bc0d7118966f7a42e0de.jpg)

Earlier in the pandemic recovery, the labor differential signaled one of the strongest labor markets since 1967, with the index remaining above 30 for 29 consecutive months. During that time, the unemployment rate remained below 4% for 30 straight months—the longest run since the late 1960s. While the labor differential has eased modestly on net in recent months, it improved to 24.0 in the June survey.

Figure 2 plots the monthly data for the job differential and the unemployment rate; note that the survey data were only collected bi-monthly from March 1967 through April 1977. We also exclude the 2020 and 2021 observations as the relationship briefly broke down with the pandemic surge in unemployment. Figure 2 shows fitted regression lines for simple linear (orange) and quadratic (green) specifications. The former implies the unemployment rate should be running between 4.0% and 4.2% (given the standard error of the regression), while the latter suggests between 4.2% and 4.3%.

Figure 2: Labor differential and unemployment rate Regression liries estimated over Jan 1967 to Jun 2024, excludes 2020-2
![](images/d3f969a5b40cb93bd2a5151ee832cf8f16b11aae2ab31ec77715c95bfac8a408.jpg)

The observations since January 2022 are shown in a brighter blue color in Figure 2. These tend to sit at the lower end of the historical distribution, suggesting some time variation in the relationship. A simple regression using the data since January 2022 finds a labor differential of 24 implies a 95% likelihood that the unemployment rate is between 3.7% and 3.9%—lower than now, which hints that a possible shift in the recent relationship could be underway.

We can generalize this idea via a “local regression” (or “lowess”) approach to estimate a flexible nonlinear (and nonparametric) relationship in the data. Figure 3 zooms into the part of the sample with a positive labor differential and contrasts a lowess estimate (green) with a linear one (orange). The lowess model suggests that, for values above the current 24, the unemployment rate is expected to remain in the vicinity of 4% and vary little. But as the labor differential declines from its current level, the estimated rise in the unemployment rate is more rapid than in the linear model. At a value of 15—still a favorably elevated labor differential historically—the lowess model suggests an unemployment rate between 4.6% and 4.7%. While not a particularly high rate, it would nonetheless raise concerns about a break in the health of the labor market. A drop in the labor differential below 10—still a solid reading—would point to an unemployment rate approaching 5%.

![](images/e69eccc12b273992f3ba14f94881c0f11aa7f4fa4e55c830cd850f8ebe080654.jpg)

## Euro Area

• Final PMI confirms a slowing in June

• Core price momentum slows in June, but more slowing in services needed to reassure on disinflation

• ECB commentary still suggests a September cut

• German industry data remain soft for now

This week’s data have raised the likelihood of the ECB delivering a second rate cut in September. The PMI was confirmed in this week’s final report for June to have stepped down from a 1.5%ar to a 1.1%ar in terms of its signal for GDP. All else equal, this reduces risks to inflation from the labor market and from companies’ ability to expand their profit margins. Separately, the flash HICP for June showed a significant slowing in core price gains from a 0.29%m/m, sa, average from January to May to a smaller increase of 0.22%m/m, sa, in June. While this is only one data print, the momentum in terms of the %3m, saar, has already moderated in recent months (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Euro area core inflation
%oya and %3m saar, dotted is ECB target for headline inflation
![](images/3a62b66206c04d5605e98f37327b4c74f6e91244fba1ce3d9cf4c71195770e7f.jpg)

The composition of the recent core inflation data is still less convincing. Core goods inflation has been weak, with outright declines in prices in the spring, followed by a gain of 0.11%m/m, sa, in June. However, services momentum is still much higher, despite a smaller gain in services prices of 0.27%m/m, sa, in June (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Euro area core inflation %3m saar
![](images/88ac42ca8dbd1fb23797ed63db00578b63d11ee881407bd9b769bc41f34326a8.jpg)

Nevertheless, the combination of more trend-like growth and some slowing in sequential inflation momentum is supportive of our call that the ECB will cut rates further in September. While unemployment remains very low, an easing in inflation momentum can come from a fading of certain pressures. For example, insurance prices have increased very strongly earlier this year and that may just be a level adjustment higher in their price level. There is probably also still scope for some normalization in tourism-related inflation. In any case, further validation of this narrative will be important in the coming months.

## ECB minutes show more balanced debate

The ECB’s June rate cut felt rushed to us as data had surprised on the upside in the run-up to the meeting and as the ECB’s inflation forecasts were revised up. Hence, that only one Executive Board member objected to the cut was a surprise. But, this week’s accounts of the June meeting rebalance this impression somewhat. “Some members” felt that the data between April and June had not led to an increase in confidence but rather an increase in uncertainty in the inflation outlook. They did feel that the disinflation forecast was intact in the “bigger picture”, but that delivering a cut in June was not “fully in line with the principle of data-dependence” and that there was a case for keeping rates on hold at the meeting. For them, the “last mile” was still potentially problematic. Nevertheless, all but one of them were willing to support the cut despite these misgivings.

For the majority, progress was still being made on all three of Lagarde’s tests, even if the strength of the services inflation data was noted. Ultimately, the decision to cut or hold comes down to judgment. On this, the meeting account had a relatively long section on the concept of data-dependence. It was argued that this is not about individual data points but about the “bigger picture” (i.e., how the data affect the three tests) and to some extent risk management. On the bigger picture, it was noted that the data did not need to move in a linear way and that the fact that the 2H25 staff forecast had been anchored very close to 2% gave confidence that current bumps were not challenging the endpoint. On risk management, “there appeared to be little risk of needing to reverse [a June cut] unless a large external shock occurred, in which case such a policy reversal could be convincingly justified”. Hence, cutting rates by 25bp in June was seen as “robust” across a wide range of scenarios and also offered “protection” against downside shocks by reducing the amount of restriction.

Going forward, the meeting account was very explicit in having no pre-commitment for upcoming meetings. Hence, the message is that the ECB had enough confidence to deliver a June cut, but would now be cautious and patient, and keep

full optionality. This may be an implicit admission that the signal ahead of the June meeting was too strong, even if the latest data (PMI, June HICP) do not make it look like a policy mistake. Overall, these sentiments were echoed in this week’s commentary at the ECB Forum in Sintra, which kept September on the table but in a more data-dependent and open-minded way.

## Euro area PMI stepped down in June

Revisions in this week’s final PMI for June were upwards but small and therefore left the composite output index 1.2pt lower than in May. At least three issues are worth noting. First, the declines are confirmed to have been driven mainly by manufacturing, which saw its output index drop 3.3pts versus a 0.5pt decline in services. Second, the employment index fell as much as the output index, and hence the PMI composite productivity index remained weak (Figure x). This implies that pressure on unemployment remains on the downside even if this week’s report for May showed a 38,000 increase in the number of unemployed. Third, the country details showed the smallest decline in the composite PMI in France (-0.1pt), the largest in Germany (-2pts) and with Italy and Spain in the middle at around -1pt. But, considering that the French composite PMI had already declined by almost 2pts in May, while most other countries increased, the French PMI is arguably underperforming, possibly reflecting election-related uncertainty.

Figure 3: Euro area labour productivity
![](images/4de199d1fb0718a3d4fdc070d3ce141a92a6394a4fd637759deba7a0459acdf1.jpg)

## Holiday timing hits the IP data

National IP reports look weak in May. Germany reported a decline of 2.5%m/m for total IP and France reported a decline of 2.1%m/m. In Germany, the VDA data jumped in April, slumped in May and recovered in June, which points to holiday-related distortions. That IP was unusually weak across many parts of manufacturing also points in that direction. The situation was more explicit in France, where the statistics office assumed that May 10 was a working day but noted that many people likely took the day off following the bank holidays on May 8 and 9. As a result, a recovery is likely in June, as shown already by the profile of the VDA data.

In an underlying sense, the manufacturing PMI still points to contractions. In Germany, industrial orders also disappointed, although the decline on an ex-bulk basis in May came after a larger increase and therefore the recent trend still looks flat (Figure 4). Even the IP data have looked more stable recently and may continue to do so if the data rebound in June due to the holiday effect reversing. The bigger problem however is that the manufacturing PMI remains weak and still points to underlying declines (Figure 5). As a result, we remain cautious about the manufacturing sector for now, even if the data had entered 2Q24 with a positive trajectory.

Figure 4: German industrial indicators
![](images/50d1758f4948699c46ee068ea9f30d9b09cd393499c3c0c5ac4d4297841c5e37.jpg)

Figure 5: German manufacturing output DI, sa
![](images/8e2e7f05921110480ea1000b47ef434da0abb401def2b111ec81706088636a3c.jpg)
Source: Destatis, Markit, J.P. Morgan

## Euro area retail sales begin to trend up

While Euro area consumer spending has already been increasing in recent quarters, this has been hard to track in the monthly data, especially retail sales. However, the trend in this week’s report through May is starting to look upwards. Euro area retail sales rose 0.1%m/m in May, reversing some of the 0.2%m/m decline in April, but this comes after after a 0.7%m/m increase in March and leaves the data tracking a gain of around 1%ar so far in 2Q24. In our view, a pickup in consumer spending would be consistent with the constellation of strong wage growth, firm employment growth and lower headline inflation, which is boosting real wages. But, the strength of the pickup is still hard to gauge given that the saving rate has moved around a lot in recent quarters (and has remained elevated overall).

The Euro area unemployment rate was unchanged at 6.4% in May, as expected. The number of unemployed increased by 38,000 however, whereas we had expected a further decline. This may suggest an easing in the labor market, but such changes can often get revised away and the PMI employment index still points to a solid trend in hiring. As a result, we would still describe the labor market data as solid.

## Japan

Corporates appear resilient in the face of rising input costs and sluggish demand

• Tankan survey suggest labor market tightness back to 1990s extremes

• Consumer confidence ticking higher from low level

• Next week: May wage growth picks up

The incoming sentiment data suggest corporates and households remain resilient in the face of multiple headwinds. For corporates, a rising wage bill, tepid real demand, and elevated input costs amid continued yen depreciation point to lingering drags. But while firms downgraded their profit growth expectations in this week’s June BoJ Tankan survey, sentiment remained relatively firm across the board. Divergence between large and small firms remains stark, however, in part reflecting varied ability to pass on increasingly sharp rises in input and labor costs. And these constraints appear to be intensifying: the index for firms’ perception of excess labor availability slipped to its lowest level since 1991.

Consumers also appear to be turning a corner slowly after several quarters of tepid activity. June consumer sentiment ticked up for the first time in three months, albeit from relatively depressed levels, likely supported by expectations for higher wages and one-off tax rebates. And alongside rising private sector confidence, inflation expectations continue to move higher. Household inflation expectations remained elevated in June, and corporate inflation expectations rose for the most part. Most measures are still trending around, or above, the 2% target; this should give BoJ cover to press on.

## BoJ Tankan sends upbeat signal

Corporate sentiment was firm in the Bank of Japan’s June Tankan Survey. The all-firms current sentiment index was unchanged at 12, while the outlook index ticked up a point to 10 from its March level. Sentiment among both manufacturers and non-manufacturers rose a point to 5 and 19, respectively (Figure 1). These outcomes were broadly in line with consensus expectations, and a touch stronger than our forecasts.

While sentiment among large manufacturers rose 2 points to 13, with a solid pickup in their outlook, small firms’ sentiment was unchanged at -1. Non-manufacturing sentiment remained robust across the board, with large firms in particular reporting upbeat conditions: the index slipped a point to 33, only a touch off record highs. Capacity constraints appear to be intensifying, suggesting that input costs may continue to rise. Firms’ perception of labor shortages slipped three points, deteriorating to its lowest level since 1991; perceptions of

equipment shortages also fell 3 points (Figure 2).

Figure 1: BoJ Tankan business sentiment, large firms DI, boxes denote outlook for Sep 2024
![](images/aa056092f71a21f5e68e436eddd444a89043528f2f54b20077f3a6215ebc1f67.jpg)

Figure 2: Firms' perception about labor and capacity DI, "excess" minus "shortage"
![](images/a80f8bc00d2c4d27778512b9137921cb577a0b435e7ee27f9834820a327c087b.jpg)

Figure 3: Japan All-industry corporate inflation expectations %p.a.
![](images/bb8d5d191c0fee41d3001680ec0317f998b2c4ca209ddbd77c16466df70664c4.jpg)

Alongside continued pressure from rising input costs, expectations for FY24 profit growth deteriorated. Large firms now expect profits to fall 8.6%oya against an estimate of -3.7% in March. We suspect part of the downgrade reflects a cut to the profit outlook for some auto makers given production suspensions. It is also worth noting that corporate sentiment around the yen remains conservative: the average forecasted USD/ JPY rate remains well below spot (firms expect a rate of almost 145 in FY24, only a slight change to their 141 prediction in March). Thus, profit growth in yen terms continues to look biased to the upside. Corporate capex plans remain upbeat, with a large upgrade (from 4%oya to around 11%) in large firms’ investment spending forecasts, and a more modest upgrade to small firms’ plans.

Corporate inflation expectations remained firm across the board, with 1-year ahead expectations unchanged at 2.4%oya. 3-year and 5-year expectations both rose 10bp to 2.3% and 2.2%, respectively; both are at or slightly above decade highs (Figure 3). Inflation expectations continue to trend around, or above, the 2% target; this should give BoJ cover to press on with policy normalization.

## Consumer sentiment ticking higher

The June consumer sentiment rose 0.2pt to 36.4, after having declined for two consecutive months in April and May (Figure 4). This is a positive sign, but the 0.2pt recovery was modest compared with a total 3.3pts decline in the earlier two months. We think the June improvement in consumer sentiment was largely attributable to the government’s one-off tax cut.

Sub-indices showed the increase in nominal income and durable goods purchases led to the June improvement. Among all sub-indices, the income growth DI rose the most in June, rising 0.7pt to 40.6 . Reflecting the recent rising expectation for higher nominal wages, the income growth DI has been trending up since last year, and the index stayed around the highest levels since 2019. The timing to buy durable goods DI also rose in June, by 0.6pt to 29.6, but the improvement came from a depressed level. We think the improvement in durable goods purchases was largely attributable to the one-off tax cuts; some households seem willing to allocate the temporary increase in income to purchase durable goods. In June, weakness arose from the labor market conditions DI and the standard of living DI. The standard of living DI has been lackluster since early 2022, reflecting higher inflation. And we expect this to remain depressed for this quarter and the next, until real income growth to turn to positive in 4Q. Indeed, consumer’s inflation expectations remained elevated even in June (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Japan Consumer sentiment
![](images/cee37f2261dbc45fb261209a057df38f2e2df3b3fb76306f828760c6c92d3258.jpg)

Figure 5: Japan Consumers' inflation expectations
![](images/a313c2192823b093b6ae4aae634c1eadee10bd3b8db9c68e09486a9a19ac6de8.jpg)

We continue to expect real consumption to recover in 2Q, albeit at a modest pace. Rising prospects for higher nominal wages and gradual fading of real income drags likely will support a gradual consumption recovery in 2Q, after having declined for four consecutive quarters through 1Q. However, we expect we will need to wait until later this year before consumption gains strong momentum backed by a firm income backdrop.

## Consumption on track for 2Q recovery

The BoJ’s Consumption Activity Index (CAI, travel balance adjusted) moved sideways in May, leaving the Apr-May average 3.1%q/q, saar above 1Q . However, overall momentum was not particularly weak, as the May print came after a solid April gain, which was revised up to 1.0%m/m, sa, from 0.5% reported earlier.

Despite lingering real income drags, we now start seeing some signs of modest recovery in consumption momentum. Consumer sentiment has improved in June, after two consecutive monthly declines, reflecting some positive prospects for higher wages and the government’s one-off tax cuts starting from June. We continue to expect GDP-based real consumption to recover in 2Q24, rising 1.5%q/q, saar, after having marked four consecutive quarterly declines since 2Q23 (Figure 6).

Figrue 6: Real consumption %q/q, saar, box denotes JPM forecasts
![](images/1e964de4e45f176f7a2af9724e4803edb57eecd2a186a56d85a3ec170a2daf2b.jpg)

## Canada

• June employment unexpectedly weakened...

• … but wage growth firmed and remains uncomfortably strong

Trade data suggest net exports will be a drag on real GDP growth in 2Q24

The June Labor Force Survey was a surprise all around, but not in a way that made the Bank of Canada’s job any easier. Job growth slowed more than expected and the unemployment rose another 0.2%-pt to a two-and-a-half year high of 6.4%. Yet wage growth firmed on the month, with the yearago pace of average hourly earnings accelerating to 5.4% from 5.1% in May for all workers, and to 5.6% from 5.2% for permanent employees. The disappointingly weak employment data—employment fell by 1,400 against expectations for a much firmer gain, with a larger decline in full-time jobs— alongside the disappointing strong wage growth figures presents conflicting signals to the Bank of Canada.

The economy appears to be slowing more than anticipated as incoming migrants and young workers are struggling to find work while facing cost-of-living pressures. Meanwhile, the upside surprise in wage growth follows an upside surprise to May CPI last week, and suggests a bumpy road to rein in inflation (Figure 1). The BoC will see one more inflation report before the Governing Council convenes later this month, but this was the final labor market update before the next policy meeting. We continue to see the BoC remaining on hold this month with one more 25bp rate cut this year likely in 4Q, as inflation gradually returns to target and the BoC remains cautious.

Figure 1: Core services CPI and average hourly earnings %3m, saar; sa by J.P. Morgan Core
![](images/a1b5d9b5d5c3fcb5634a8155d545749a3a48ea9a15a5e7c8c5a54cefcc0943e7.jpg)

In the details of this week’s jobs report, overall job losses were led by a drop in full-time employment (-3,400); parttime work rose by 1,900 in June. The participation rate also ticked down while the employment rate (employees as a share of the over-15 population) fell for the eighth time in nine months. Coming into the report, growth in goods sector

employment had softened somewhat this year while job gains in the services sector remained strong by broad historical standards. June’s decline in employment reflected somewhat of a departure from this general pattern, with job losses concentrated in the services sector (-14,100) while the goods sector (+12,600) provided a partial offset. Transportation and warehousing, information and recreation, and wholesale and retail trade all declined in June among services; accommodation and food services jobs increased. Goods sector employment last month was boosted by a decent gain in manufacturing and a solid rise in agricultural employment.

## Trade likely a drag on growth in 2Q

This week’s May international merchandise trade report suggested that trade was likely a more significant drag on growth last quarter than previously anticipated. Nominal exports fell 2.6% in May while imports moved down 1.6% on the month. As a result, the nominal merchandise trade deficit widened to C\$1.9bil in May from C\$1.3bil in April. With data on trade in goods for April and May in hand, net exports are now tracking a larger drag on real GDP growth in 2Q24. That said, there is a fair bit of volatility in the trade data, and we have limited visibility on the services side of the trade balance.

According to our estimates, total exports in volume terms declined 2.2% in May, and are tracking a sizable 6.8%ar contraction for 2Q overall (Figure 2). Total imports in volume terms receded 1.2% in May, and are tracking a more modest 0.5% contraction for last quarter. In the details, the major trade categories of energy products and autos saw mixed changes in May. A 3.4% rise in real exports of motor vehicles and parts provided a partial offset to the overall decrease in total exports. Real exports of energy products edged up 0.4% in May. Meanwhile, real imports of both energy products and motor vehicles and parts contributed to the overall dip in total imports in May, falling 5.7% and 4.3%, respectively.

Figure 2: Real merchandise exports and imports %3m/3m, saar; chained 2017 C\$
![](images/1df79a6871a7f7339fef3eef2b5904aa265234388faf6a491b33197f61dbb52c.jpg)

## Mexico

• Next week Banxico minutes to elaborate on the dovish stance from June

We expect a more balanced narrative that digs into improving inflation dynamics and worsening growth conditions

Inflation in June to evidence next week contrasting forces between benign core and deteriorating non-core

• Also next week, industrial production for May

Next week, on Thursday, minutes to the June policy decision in which Banxico kept rates unchanged at 11% will be released. As broadly expected, a majority of board members endorsed the on-hold decision, with Mr. Omar Mejía dissenting in favor of a 25bp cut.

While we did not foresee an open disagreement with the rest of the COPOM, the fact that inflation continues to show benign dynamics in its all-important core component and that tight policy conditions remain in place (Figure 1) suggested the statement would be a “dovish-on-hold.”

Indeed, the discussion, in our view, was broadly dovish, suggesting the board is ready to resume cuts at its next August meeting. Beyond the emphasis on the disinflation gains, the board also jumped on board the “growth concerns” train, something that we expected to occur in May when we entertained, wrongly, a consecutive cut after the March 25bp reduction in the policy rate.

The board did notice the underwhelming growth dynamics and downgraded its growth outlook—now openly underscoring downside risks to economic activity.

We expect the minutes to elaborate on both disinflation forces and some of the upside risks embedded in the ongoing MXN re-adjustment, and the forces behind growth disappointments. The Fed factor, as we have mentioned, probably will remain backstage but is expected to appear at some point in the discussion.

We believe, in any case, that the minutes will be more neutral considering the debates related to risks associated to fiscal and institutional challenges. Both are expected to keep the currency more sensitive than before, increasing the risk of upward pressures to tradeable goods and more specifically, core goods inflation.

Figure 1: Ex-ante policy rate vs inflation
![](images/8025ced9112e04f876af665b7cf484551b8d791986065212a5133da51bf6177b.jpg)

## Core falling in line in spite of headline spike

Both gradual disinflation and weaker growth dynamics could be challenged this week, but we think both should be put in context. On the CPI front we do look for an eye-catching pickup in headline inflation to just above 5%oya (5.04%) in June 2H (4.91%oya for June as a whole). This, however, would be solely driven by food prices spiking, in particular of key items of the food CPI basket such as tomatoes.

Looking through the noise in these prices—which cannot be entirely downplayed given what appears to have been an increase in persistence of supply shocks that used to raise prices only transitorily—the fact is that core inflation is showing slow but sure signs of improvement (Figure 2). Core CPI is expected to run around 0.31%m/m, sa, with services at 0.4%m/m, sa, which is still fairly high.

Figure 2: Stability of inflation components in June
![](images/6d65396f2dfe3da018699fbf9527a043665fbe72ce9c99812d595e63b697612a.jpg)

That said, the underlying non-tourism services category (“other services” minus tourism) is likely to confirm the big downturn (Figure 3) signaled in the preliminary report for the month.

As we have elaborated before (see here), while the mediumterm outlook is for average higher inflation vs. pre-pandemic, the short-term outlook is encouraging and in line with our call for underlying inflation to continue to slow.

Figure 3: Quarterly Core inflation path %oya
![](images/3cb80aabee3cc4c0924cbdbb28980443fe58e416e57ea5169664d59da5add19f.jpg)
Source: INEGI, Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Last update for Banxico Jun 27, 2024

## Good IP data amid weakening conditions

On the activity front, the April showers should give way to some green shoots in the May industrial production data. We look for IP to bounce back 0.8%m/m. This would leave IP on track for a modest 1.5%ar gain in 2Q, reinforcing downside risks to our forecast for a 3.5% annualized gain in last quarter’s GDP.

On the brighter side, after slipping in April, manufacturing looks set to bounce back 0.6%m/m, the same as in Feb/Mar (Figure 4). Meanwhile, construction should maintain its recent upswing and deliver a robust 3.7%m/m gain. That said, most of the thrust to construction should come from a big jump in public works ahead of the June 1 election (22%m/m, sa as per our seasonal adjustment), and the sector could cool again in 2H.

Our main concern is that 2H24 construction is expected to turn south as most priority-projects from the government will be completed. Downside risks to full-year growth (currently at 1.8%y/y) are also in place. Finally, mining, which similarly to utilities has evolved in a see-saw pattern for a while, likely remained a drag (-1.1%m/m).

Figure 4: Manufacturing production and new orders PMI
![](images/fa60d895df7afa75edeb666ea8e9947b9277698ca2822384f00078a61ed6965d.jpg)

## Brazil

Despite encouraging data, fiscal concerns led expectations to worsen, particularly in the last month

This led to a debate of whether BCB will make a U-turn and start hiking this year

• While acknowledging that chances of hikes have risen, we still expect stable policy rate ahead

• This week’s announcement of 0.2% of GDP in federal spending cuts reinforces our view

Brazilian financial markets are in the middle of a storm that has driven the exchange rate to be among the worst performers of the year—particularly in June—across major currencies. While part of this move is global, the bulk of the BRL excess depreciation is idiosyncratic. Fundamentally, data are unchanged and aligning with our expectations since: past inflation was fairly benign, economic growth looks robust, and external accounts are healthy. But the expectations about the future of the Brazilian economy are deteriorating, with high public expenditures growth and the government seemingly doing nothing to curb it, challenging its own fiscal framework that sets real expenditures growth between 0.6% and 2.5% until the end of 2026.

This backdrop is fueling a rise in inflation expectations, which has shifted the debate about monetary policy. At the start of the year the discussion was on how low the policy rate could go, with markets pricing a terminal rate well below our expectations. Now, the debate is on whether BCB will need to hike rates in this second half of the year and beyond to contain inflationary pressures led by excessive fiscal stimulus and FX overshoot. In our view, the central bank will keep rates unchanged at 10.50% going forward, supported by the easing cycle abroad. Similarly, COPOM board members have been disavowing the market pricing of hikes, with Governor Campos affirming in a panel with Chair Powell and ECB’s governor Lagarde this week that the markets were out of sync with reality. Their comments follow the board’s decision to show their reference scenario in June, one where stable rates until the end of 2025 would be enough to put inflation at 3.1%, very close to the mid-point of BCB’s target.

All that said, to assess the odds of a reversal in the monetary policy cycle, we use BCB’s main tool in the policymaking process: its projections for the relevant horizon. At the current level of the exchange rate, of inflation expectations, and the policy rate, we calculate that BCB’s projection for 2025 CPI tends to move further away from the target, from 3.1% in the last meeting to 3.3% currently (Table 1). This would suggest rising chances of rate hikes ahead, especially if inflation expectations continue trending up. However, even if 2025 is

the central bank’s main target at this point of the year, the 2026 inflation projection will become increasingly important in the second half of 2024, and the central bank’s model would likely point to inflation close to the target two years from now (Table 2).

Table 1: COPOM 2025 inflation scenario - BCB's model\*
%oya 2025 CPI. Assuming SELIC rate at 10.50%

Table 2: COPOM 2026 inflation scenario - BCB's model\*

<table><tr><td>BRL/2026 expectations</td><td>3.60</td><td>3.80</td><td>4.00</td></tr><tr><td>5.80</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td>5.70</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td>5.50</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td></tr><tr><td>5.30</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.0</td></tr><tr><td>5.20</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.0</td></tr><tr><td>5.00</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.9</td></tr></table>

\*Based on estimates of the elasticities in BCB's model

In the end, the monetary policy decisions seem to crucially depend on where expectations land after the recent storm. As we have defended in the past, government announcements that strengthen the commitment to a fiscal adjustment with spending measures seem vital to contain the exchange rate depreciation and the rise of inflation expectations.

An important step in this direction was taken this week. After a meeting with President Lula and key cabinet members, Finance Minister Fernando Haddad announced a BRL25.9bn budget cut for 2025, anticipating increased savings due to a review of social benefits (“pente-fino”). The announcement is more important for signaling commitment to the fiscal framework than for the number itself, as not much detail was given on how this revision will work. According to our estimates, reducing spending on social security by this much would require an effort equivalent to the 2016-2018 review, when over 1.1 trillion cases were put under scrutiny and several hundred thousand benefits were suspended. While awaiting more details, we stand by our view that a BRL10-20bn reduction over time is more likely and that budget freezes of BRL15-20bn should be enacted in the near term. Furthermore, we caution that the government’s commitment to the fiscal framework and the balanced budget target will be tested on August 31, when the 2025 budget proposal must be delivered (Table 3).

## Impact of floods on IP was limited in May

We were expecting IP to fall 3%m/m, sa in May, driven by the effect of the floods in Rio Grande do Sul, a state we estimate accounts for 7% of the national industrial production. However, IP fell only 0.9%m/m, sa, partly on the strength in mining industry—Rio Grande do Sul doesn't have a meaningful mining sector—which rose 2.6%m/m, sa with the recovery of oil production in the month following months of weakness. By contrast, the manufacturing production that was recovering since the beginning of the year was more significantly hit by the floods, falling 2.2%m/m, sa in May. The state-by-state breakdown should be available next week, giving a clearer picture of the total impact of the floods.

Because of falling mining production in the year through April, mainly on the back of temporary stoppages, industrial production should be negative in 2Q. But, considering that the mining sector should recover, and the manufacturing sector is showing resilience even with the (also temporary) flood impact, we are encouraged about the industrial sector performance ahead. Sentiment indicators remain robust in the manufacturing sector, with the PMI stabilizing above 52 and FGV industrial confidence moving consistently up (Figure 1).

Figure 1: PMI manufacturing and FGV manufacturing confidence
![](images/cf3888d76c882e6b10312a3201f52c7112e030b91083676a53eeaefbe0a0ab1b.jpg)

The nowcaster fell from 3.1% to 2.5%, but remains significantly above our call for a 1% expansion in 2Q GDP, an estimate downgraded last month assuming a drag from the floods that halved our initial expectations.
