## Argentina

Real activity nearly flat in April; annual GDP forecast maintained at -3.4%y/y

• The Blend FX has materially limited the ability to accumulate reserves faster

• And the debt service scheduled will weigh on net reserves accumulation in 3Q

• Real tax collection landed back in negative terrain in June (-13.9%oya)

The approval of the fiscal package secure additional revenues worth 0.7% of GDP; watch for the new pension formula

## Real activity nearly flat in April

Real activity contracted just slightly in April, with the monthly activity indicator falling by 0.1%m/m, sa. Regarding ex. agriculture activity, we estimate it contracted by 0.2%m/m, sa in the month, which compares to a -0.5% average for the prior 3 months. The decoupling of headline and ex. agricultural activity becomes more visible on an over-year-ago basis, as the recovery of agri conditions prove a boost for growth on base effects, largely masking the over-year-ago contraction of ex. primary production. Indeed, headline activity printed -1.7%oya in April, with ex.agriculture printing -5.3%oya.

When compared to recent activity collapses, the recession at this stage has proved more costly than the 2018 sudden stop. Yet, the April performance suggests that the overall drop is to prove shallower (Figure 1). That said, it’s worth noting that the current economic activity level is below those observed in 2018 and similar to 2012’s lows.

Figure 1: Comparing the current recession to prior events Real activity, index, sa, (t=0) pre recession peak level =100
![](images/d07d873f465c9a34d11724833723f3c2fba362d1c07d5e47767c86af02d976d1.jpg)

Ahead, our base case is for activity growth to stabilize in the second quarter, with sequential GDP turning a bit into positive terrain (+0.5%q/q, saar) after the collapse in 1Q (-9.9%ar). We maintain the annual GDP forecast at -3.4%y/y. So far, the incoming high frequency data have proved a mixed bag. Fiscal revenues (deflated by CPI) gained momentum in May, though VAT collection plunged. Auto production dropped in May after showing a gain in April, while cement sales started to recover. Seasonally adjusted imports gained, while exports dropped in May.

## Net reserves poised to decline in 3Q

Regarding the international reserves accumulation pace, in June the BCRA sold US\$128mn in the official FX market, the first negative month since the Milei administration took office. The fact that 20% of the export inflows are being supplied to the financial FX (around US\$7.8bn between December and May, by our estimates)—compounded by below-historical-average inflows from the cereal complex, the ongoing normalization of imports payments (Figure 2), and likely higher demand for energy imports due to the adverse cold conditions—has limited the ability to accumulate reserves on the month. In sync with lower reserves accumulation, the FX gap has continued to widen, despite the recent monetary policy announcements (see note), reaching 54% by July 1, from 44% a month ago.

Figure 2: Non-energy imports commercial debt
![](images/8be515a9999cdd0b653319ef764ea14441d273cdc1ea2687b334a133dc46a2a3.jpg)
Source: BCRA and J.P. Morgan

Going forward, monitoring the evolution of the exporters supply, not only from the agri sector but also from the rest of the sectors (particularly energy), will be key to assessing the limits of the current policy framework, especially when taking into account the 40% YTD appreciation in exporters’ real effective exchange rate. Moreover, it will also be important to monitor inflows under the regime for large investments (known as ‘RIGI’) and tax amnesty once the Omnibus bill and fiscal package are regulated (likely starting in mid-August), as they could help narrow the FX gap.

In our estimates, net FX reserves stood at -US\$0.7bn as of June 23 (note our estimate considers as hard-currency liabilities Bopreal series 2 amortizations of US\$2.1bn in the next 12 months but not Treasury deposits). Meanwhile, USD liquid reserves stood at around US\$6.5bn (from almost null when the administration took office).

We expect export inflows in the official FX market of around US\$32bn in the next six months, under the assumption that the Blend-FX (80% at the official market and 20% at the financial FX) will remain in place, as stated recently by the authorities. Of note, should the Blend-FX be eliminated, export inflows in the official FX market could increase up to US\$8bn in 2H24. Meanwhile, the import outflows are expected at US\$27bn, assuming an increase in imports in 4Q amid a soft economic activity improvement and the reduction of the PAIS tax.

But the government will face US\$2.7bn in debt service with the market in July (US\$1.2bn in capital amortizations and the rest in interests). Moreover, there are net payments to the IMF for another US\$0.6bn and Bopreal series 2 capital amortizations for US\$1.2bn until December. Regarding other IFIs, there is debt service for US\$2.7bn between July and December, which we assume is fully financed. Of note, so far this year there has not been financing to roll over IFI’s debt.

Against this backdrop, we expect BCRA net reserves to decline by around US\$1.5bn between July and December (-US\$2bn in 3Q, then to recover partially in 4Q), implying an accumulation of around US\$7.5bn in net reserves in full-year 2024, given the good performance during 1H24. This is in line with the US\$7bn net reserves accumulation target as per the IMF program and would be consistent with net reserves closing the year at around -US\$2bn. We believe advancing toward the release of capital controls is paramount to secure and accelerate the ability to replenish reserves in the medium term.

## June tax collection saw payback

As expected, real tax collection landed back in negative terrain after the strong boost observed last month. Indeed, tax revenues declined by 13.9%oya in real terms in June, from +12%oya reported last month and the -5.1%oya average through May. YTD, real tax collection is down by 6.8%oya. On a sequential basis, June’s real tax collection (J.P. Morgan’s seasonal adjustment) also printed in the red, at -21.9%m/m, sa. But the monthly decline was explained by the payback from the record-high income tax collection seen last month on year-end FX devaluation. Indeed, when controlling for the impact of the income tax collection, real revenues remained flat in June.

In the breakdown by revenue type, the “PAIS tax” has continued to support tax collection YTD, increasing by 281%oya, and representing 6.4% of total tax collection in 1H24, from just 1.6% in same period last year. On a sequential basis, the PAIS tax revenues increased again in June when adjusting for seasonality, by 15.1%m/m, as tax collection related to the late-May Bopreal issuance impacted June’s tax revenues given the extension of the payment deadline. Meanwhile, foreign-trade tax collection declined for the third consecutive month in sequential terms, though it has increased by 16.2% YTD on strong 1Q24 performance following December 23 FX devaluation. Last, consumption-related VAT revenues rebounded on the month (+4.9%m/m), with momentum at +11%3m/3m, saar.

As we highlighted in our recent notes, the approvals of the Omnibus bill and fiscal package are key milestones as they secure additional revenues of 0.7% of GDP in our estimates through year-end, which are paramount in the transition toward an economic activity rebound. The new revenues will start flowing once the bills are regulated (likely in 2H August). That said, we now watch to see the fate of the new pension formula, which was approved in the Lower House last month, and has started being discussed in Senate commissions this week. The formula, among other changes, includes an additional 7.2% increase in pensions to compensate for January’s real loss, carrying a fiscal cost of about 0.4% of GDP this year, but which could increase up to 1.8% of GDP in the years to come, in our estimates, if real wages return to the levels seen in 2017 on average (note that the formula mandates an annual benefit increase equivalent to half the difference between the growth in nominal wages and annual inflation, if wages increase more than inflation).

## Andeans

Chile: May headline activity slipped by 0.4%m/m, sa, and ex. mining 0.6%

• We maintain the call for sequential activity to accelerate in 3Q

Colombia: The Board, by majority, decided to cut the policy rate by 50bp, to 11.25%

• Our base case is for the BanRep to continue reducing the policy rate by 50bp this month

• The policy rate is projected at 8.5% by December

## Chile: May activity disappoints

Economic activity disappointed in May. The monthly headline activity index slipped by 0.4%m/m, sa, on the heels of April’s 0.3% contraction.

The more granular analysis shows goods production down by 0.4%m/m, sa, driven by manufacturing (2.3%m/m). But commerce and services also dropped, by 0.4%m/m and 0.2%, respectively.

When the last three-month sequential performance is analyzed, upon revisions, headline activity ran at just +0.4%3m/3m, saar, decelerating from the strong +7.5%ar logged in the first quarter of the year. In all, by May headline activity had gained a still good +1.3%,sa, compared to December’s level.

As usual, we focus on ex. mining activity, the most relevant cyclical indicator. Ex.mining slipped by 0.6%m/m, sa, after a flat print in April (Figure 1). The sequential pace on a threemonth basis thus moved into negative territory, to -0.5%3m/3m, saar, after posting 4.5%ar in the first quarter of the year—the strongest quarterly expansion pace since late 2021.

Figure 1: Chile economic activity
![](images/508f3c47dad8a876eb769f6fcef7d2c4604e5f579d1d618e886585fe6d91227d.jpg)
Source CBC and J.P. Morgan

In all, the published 2Q activity data continue to align with our base case for a quarterly sequential contraction, albeit a more pronounced one than we had forecasted. The weak and broad-based ex.mining performance in May thus prompts a revision lower of 2Q24 GDP, to -2.3%ar, from -0.9% previously. Note the quarterly sequential contraction would follow the well-above-potential performance observed in the first quarter of the year, though revised to +7.5%ar (from 7.8%ar).

Despite the 2Q downward revision, we maintain a constructive view for the second half of the year, with activity gaining on average close to 3.0%ar. Thus, despite the May disappointment, and while recognizing forecast risk now skews to the downside, we maintain the 2024 annual GDP projection at +2.7%y/y, consistent with +2.9%Q4/Q4.

## Colombia: BanRep sustains easing

The Board, by majority, decided to cut the policy rate by 50bp, to 11.25%. Four directors voted in favor, while two voted for a reduction of 75bp (one director did not participate in the meeting). The Board reiterated that future decisions will depend on the new information available.

In the statement, the Board emphasized that while inflation in May remained unchanged at 7.2%, when excluding food and regulated items it decelerated to 6.1%, from 6.4%. Regarding expectations, the survey of economic analysts showed a median drop in the next 12 months, by 30bp, to 4.6%, while inflation expectations derived from market prices remained relatively stable and, although they indicate a downward trajectory over time, they remain above the target until the end of 2025. On activity, after a stronger-than-expected 1Q performance, the economy continued on its recovery trajectory in the second quarter. On the financial side, it was noted that country risk premiums and the exchange rate have increased, in a context where international financial conditions remain restrictive.

A downward trending annual inflation rate, a still high ex-ante real rate (Figure 2), and the recent fiscal adjustments allowed the monetary authority to continue the rates normalization, despite the currency slipping by almost 10% since mid-May. In our assessment the currency move has not responded to a change in fundamentals, so we maintain monetary easing ahead.

CPI
Figure 2: Colombia monetary policy rate
![](images/3d980d5182be5239b03b72a22ac7a30c213d6c1ac29d999aae83582d7f8ec813.jpg)

Our base case is for the BanRep to continue reducing the policy rate by 50bp in July, while accelerating the easing pace in the last three meetings of the year to 75bp/meeting, for the policy rate to converge to 8.5% in December. That acceleration is conditional on our below-consensus inflation forecast for 2H24 (headline CPI converging to 5.3% by December), as well international financial conditions and exchange rate behavior.

## United Kingdom

• Labour win election with a huge majority, set to stay cautious initially

• More fiscal detail to follow at a Budget later this year

We expect limited near term changes to the current fiscal path and growth

• DMP survey shows further moderation in wage growth; BoE set to cut in August

The election results show Labour winning in a landslide victory. Labour has 412 seats at the time of writing with two still to declare. The Conservatives are on 121 with the Liberal Democrats on 71. That gives Labour a majority of 174. This large majority gives the party leadership the power to stick to its central plan to offer stability, encourage private sector investment and lift longer term growth. The new government intends to implement reforms to achieve this aim, given clear fiscal constraints. But we expect it will struggle to achieve its growth objective.

The sizeable election victory masks a less convincing share of the national vote, and we expect it will come under pressure through time to take a different approach. We expect that will involve searching for leeway around the fiscal rules as currently defined, which if managed carefully would open up scope for increased public sector investment (see RN, “Exploring Labour’s subtle Plan B”). This will take time, however, and in the meantime we expect it will commit to a similar fiscal tightening as shown in the current plans when holding a Budget later this year. This will limit the near term growth implications, and we continue to expect the BoE will cut twice this year starting in August. The DMP survey on wage expectations continued to moderate this week, reinforcing the cast for a cut at the next meeting (barring a significant surprise in June inflation).

## What does a large majority mean?

A large majority will tend to limit the influence of the fringes of the political party in government, but it also hands the party significant power. Some have asked whether this could produce a leftward shift from the Labour party over time, away from the more centrist stance it has taken in the run-up to this election and closer to the party’s ideological roots.

This is a possibility, but Labour will not take the size of its lead for granted. The number of seats Labour has won is very large relative to its share of the national vote. This reflects a number of factors including the Conservative vote being split by the Reform Party, typical Conservative voters having shifted to Labour in key areas, and the quirks of the first-past-the post electoral system.

Labour’s large majority could potentially be eroded as quickly as its has been built since the 2019 election. Many voters have shifted to Labour as a protest against the Conservatives, an impact which could fade over time. And the Conservatives appear likely to shift to the right to claw back votes lost to the Reform Party, helping to rebuild some of its loss. The leadership of the Labour party will be cognisant of this. And with an ambition to remain in power for more than just one term, it will likely to be hesitant to deviate quickly from the centrist stance it has sought a mandate for.

## Steady fiscal approach, for now

Little is known about the details of Labour’s approach to fiscal policy, and this is unlikely to change in the coming weeks. The party is communicating that there will be no major tax rises, no significant spending cuts and no slippage from the current fiscal rules, all while boosting trend growth. Absent good luck, it will be difficult to achieve all these commitments over the next parliament. Labour intends to relax planning regulations and implement other reforms fairly quickly. This could spur growth to some degree, but the magnitude is likely to be small and take time to occur (see UK: An uphill struggle for Labour in government).

More will become clear at the next Budget. The OBR requires 10 weeks notice to prepare its forecasts. More detail could hence come in mid-September, but Labour may choose to wait until after its annual party conference late that month to hold the budget (as well as an overdue spending review setting out 2024/25 departmental allocations). Hence October or November might be more realistic for detailed budget plans. In the meantime, some detail is likely to be offered through policy speeches. This will include ideas that Labour has already been working on but has not yet unveiled.

Our expectation is that Labour will begin by broadly sticking to the existing overall projections that show a structural fiscal tightening of around half a percentage point of GDP next year. Within that, the composition is likely to tilt more towards tax increases rather than spending restraint relative to the current plans, but we expect it will avoid large tax increases and show restraint in spending allocations, particularly for non-protected departments.

We expect Labour will also seek to use leeway around the fis cal rules, although over time it will have to make more difficult choices which we think will involve more borrowing. Sticking to a similar aggregate fiscal path initially would have fairly limited implications for the BoE, where we expect an August cut to be followed up by another in November.

## DMP wage expectations continue to ease

The BoE’s latest DMP business survey (Figure 1) showed a further moderation in expected year ahead wage growth from 4.1% to 4.0%. The DMP has done a better job of tracking the official AWE pay data than the REC. While still high, the steady decline in the DMP of late should provide the BoE with a little more reassurance on domestic inflation pressures. It is downplaying the current strength in the recent AWE data, and barring a large surprise in the upcoming June CPI release, we expect it will cut in August.

Figure 1: Forward looking surveys of wage growth
![](images/fdaf52914cd99e2151c7d6f2a548dcf97399c58a388be08c1e2b393a680073f4.jpg)
Source: ONS, KPMG, REC, BoE

Retail sales rebounded from last month’s weather-related decline with a 2.9%m/m, sa gain in May. A recovery had looked likely but the magnitude was larger than expected. In isolation, this is worth around 0.1-0.2% on GDP for the month, via the retail distribution sector. Many other factors are at play, but weather related factors may also act to support a rebound in construction too. Any upside surprise in May GDP relative to our 0.2%m/m forecast would (unless there are notable revisions) add upside risk around our 2.0%q/q annualized forecast for 2Q GDP. This is where the BoE currently is on 2Q too.

## Emerging Europe

NBP: On hold, we revise our policy rate forecast to anticipate later rate cuts

• Türkiye: Sharp disinflation underway

• Israel: BoI to remain on hold

• Russia: Cooling not as fast as wanted

## NBP: On hold

Following an uneventful rate decision (on hold at 5.75%) and nearly carbon-copied statement, Governor Glapinski stole the show in his usual day-after press conference, by doubling down on his already hawkish stance. Although macro data keeps leaning on the dovish side (inflation has generally been better than expected, whereas activity has disappointed) the Governor now argues that rate cuts are possible in early 2026 at the earliest (before he referenced mid-2025).

Glapinski argued that on the back of “CPI-shield” removal, inflation will rise to around 5% by year-end (similar to our forecast) but after that, “many are forgetting” that energy cap measures expire at the end of the year, and hence there will be an additional bump increase in CPI in 1Q25. It is true that some of the Government’s measures expire at end-2024 and mechanically, this could trigger price increases in early 2025. However, we believe it is highly likely that energy policy supports will be extended into 2025, especially in the context of an important Presidential election.

In our recent commentary we highlighted the increasing contrast between the dovish data (inflation has generally been better than expected, whereas activity has disappointed) and the NBP’s hawkish stance, flagging that we were losing conviction in our call for rate cuts in 4Q24. While we were keen to wait on the next round of CPI data to reassess, this week’s monetary policy press conference brought an escalation in the hawkish rhetoric which led us to make a change.

From a macro standpoint, we still believe the direction of travel is towards a more dovish stance and ultimately some rate cuts, but it’s hard to fight the decision maker’s near-term preferences when there are no obvious levers to force his hand (market pressure works better in the other direction, as was seen in 2021). Therefore, we are pushing the next (25bp) rate cut to 2Q25. However, we expect that the NBP will remain hawkish even when cutting, and the next mini-cycle will be a shallow one so we maintain the end-2025 rate at 5% (Figure 1).

Figure 1: NBP interest rate outlook
![](images/c9219b3731f6b248395ea7199de704c83a5e974017076c9b105b24f9969803e6.jpg)

## Türkiye: Sharp disinflation underway

Headline CPI inflation came in at 1.6% m/m (2% sa) in June, below our expectation of 2.1% and the market consensus of 2.2%. The downside surprise mainly stemmed from clothing and services prices. Thanks to real lira appreciation and the slowdown in domestic demand, core goods prices were up only 0.1% in June, taking their over-year-ago inflation rate to 50.6% in June from 56.5% oya in May. Clothing prices fell by 0.7% m/m, leading annual clothing inflation down to 46.9% oya, from 49.9%. Services prices rose by 3.3% m/m, while over-year-ago services inflation slowed to 95.3% oya in June, from 95.9%. Restaurant hotel inflation slowed to 3.1% m/m in June (versus 5.5% in May), while rents rose 5.1% m/ m in June, from 5.5% in May. In our view, these are the first signs of slowing domestic demand on impacting core goods and services prices. Core inflation (C Index; CPI excluding energy, food, beverages, tobacco and gold) rose by 1.7% m/m in June (versus 3.8% m/m in May), leading oya core inflation down to 71.4% in June, 75.0% in May (Figure 2).

![](images/e2ba3a2b81219a218bf6b64ef863ade2784224843edce3863e23835cec5e7fc0.jpg)

The sequential pace of inflation is likely to pick up in July due to an electricity price hike of 38% and automatic tax hikes. The weight of electricity in CPI basket is 1.76%, which means direct impact of 0.67%-pt on July inflation.The special consumption tax on motor fuel prices, alcohol, and tobacco was automatically hiked this month in line with cumulative PPI inflation in 1H24. There will also be automatic interim wage hikes for pensioners and civil servants. As a result, we expect monthly inflation to pick up to 2.6% m/m in July, from 1.6% m/m in June. We expect the natural gas price to be hiked by 38% next month. The weight of natural gas in the CPI basket is 2.57%, which means 1%-pt direct impact on August inflation. We assume these energy utility price hikes are likely to be one and done for this year, which means there will be no utility price hikes in October.

In our view, headline inflation is set to fall sharply towards 50% by August and 42.5% by year-end (Figure 4). We expect headline inflation to fall towards 60% oya in July and 50% oya in August on the back of strong favorable base effects. We lower our end-2024 inflation forecast to 42.5% oya (previously 43.5%) and end-2025 inflation forecast to 25% oya (previously 25.2%) as the June downside surprise is mainly driven by core items.

Figure 3:Türkiye CPI forecast

## Israel: BoI preview

The BoI is universally expected to remain on hold next week. The MPC signalled its neutral stance at the previous meeting and the fundamental picture has not changed dramatically since then. Inflation data softened in May following upside surprises of March-April — the ups and downs were mostly driven by a few volatile items in recent months, while the underlying inflation trend has firmed following the recovery in the economy. Growth data improved in May following some softness in previous month, but risks to our 6.0%q/q, saar, forecast for 2Q still appear skewed to the downside. Fiscal dynamics have not changed much, with the main uncertainty being about the scale of further military spending and contours of fiscal adjustment in 2025.

The main obstacle for the BoI, however, remains substantial geopolitical uncertainty. Perceptions around potential war in the north have further worsened in recent weeks, weighing on markets. The BoI’s judgement will likely remain that ensuring stability should be its prevailing objective, with any discussion of potential easing to be delayed until more clarity on geopolitical front.

## Russia: Cooling not as fast as wanted

The resilience of the Russian economy to shocks, including monetary policy shocks, remains a curious phenomena. Our (and consensus) growth forecasts continue to creep higher in response to mostly upside surprises in high-frequency data. Following a somewhat softer April, May activity data again surprised to the upside — increases in output were broadbased across sectors, while the consumer (after a brief pause) started to spend again (more detail on May data here). We lift our tracking of 2Q growth 0.5pts to 1.75%q/q saar, which we expect should be close to Russian trend growth these days. Our 2024 growth forecast increases 0.2%-pt to 3.5%, our 2025 forecast remains unchanged at 1.2%; risks to both are probably still skewed slightly to the upside.

The CBR has expressed dissatisfaction about the economy’s lack of reaction to prior monetary policy tightening (highlighting the lack of response in credit growth more narrowly). The regulator has therefore continued tightening macro-prudential regulations to tame credit growth and has signalled high odds of further policy rate hikes. The latter message was strengthened further recently following a string of high-ish weekly CPI prints. Indeed, judging by weekly data, both June and July CPI will be significantly above our (and, we suspect CBR’s) original expectations, and inflation is tracking 6-7%oya for end-24 vs. the CBR’s last published forecast of 4.3-4.8. It appears likely that the CBR will revise its inflation and growth forecasts up at the upcoming July meeting and will use these revisions to justify a rate hike. We now anticipate a 100bp hike to 17% at the July meeting (risks skewed to 18%). This should be followed by a prolonged pause before policy easing could ensue from the end of this year once growth and inflation momentum moderate.

The mid-month price survey suggests some upside risks to food prices and we expect a relatively high 0.5%m/m reading, which would mark some inflection from previous negative readings. Core inflation likely was up 0.5%m/m as the month is seasonally strong as the arrival of summer promotes typical increases in holiday related items. Seasonally adjusted we expect core CPI of 0.2%m/m. If confirmed, this would result in core CPI staying flat in over-year-ago terms at 2.5%oya.

We forecast that Hungarian CPI fell slightly to 3.8%oya in June (from 4%), marking six full months inside the NBH’s target band. The main force on the downside is fuel which due to base effects is likely to have fallen to 4.4%oya (from 9.2%). Very likely the NBH’s main core CPI metric did not fall in June after 14 consecutive drops, it may actually tick higher to 4.1%oya, from 4%. The alternative measure of core excluding all food, is expected to have fallen to 5.4%oya from 5.6%.

## South Africa

• Ramaphosa announced a carefully structured GNU cabinet

• We view the appointments as well suited to execute policy initiatives

• Critical task shifts to one focused on co-operation and common ground

Weak May trade balance, but still tracks our 2Q estimate of a 1.8% of GDP surplus

A key hurdle of the Government of National Unity (GNU) formation has been cleared as President Ramaphosa announced a carefully structured cabinet. Back-and-forth over the cabinet posts had lasted two weeks and risked derailing the GNU at one point, although we believed the incentives of the DA were geared to come to a compromise. It probably also did not go unnoticed by the DA that a dialogue between (at least parts of) the ANC and the left-radical EFF and MK was again underway, with the EFF late last week making an appeal to join the GNU (if the DA was removed). Yet, the GNU as it currently stands holds 11 parties, excluding the EFF and MK, and a cabinet has now been announced. The DA was assigned six ministerial posts (out of 32) with a further seven for other non-ANC GNU members and the remainder held by the ANC. We had expected five to six posts for the DA, so this was broadly in line, particularly as the assigned posts are key posts for service delivery.

The critical task now is for GNU parties to shift from an often confrontational stance, as recently as last week, to one focused on co-operation and common ground to avert a (later) adverse outcome. To be sure, the ANC and DA managed to navigate choppy waters, but this was in the face of a political crunch time and amid a tight external timeline. We don’t think the shift to focus on co-operation and a common agenda will be an easy one, but it will be crucial in order to draw up a constructive policy and reform agenda at the upcoming strategy session. We think for now incentives remain strong to make the GNU work and to look for early wins well before the political cycle diverts incentives and intra-party dynamics become more complex (e.g. from late-25 onwards).

## External balances weakened in May

Midway into the 2Q external sector resilience appears to have cracked. The seasonally adjusted trade balance declined by 26.7%m/m to R12.8bn in May. Exports contracted by 7.3%m/ m sa in May, with the fall scattered across the major categories. Imports declined by less at 5.3%. Key exports commodity terms of trade trended stronger in May, with the weak print probably reflecting subdued volume. The trade surplus fell to 1.7% of GDP on a cumulative 3 months basis from 2.1% in

April (Table 1). Overall, the outcome would suggest upside risks to the 1.8% of GDP 2Q trade surplus (from 2.6%) estimate that underlie our forecasts for a 1.6% of GDP C/A deficit (from -1.2).

## Australia and New Zealand

The RBA minutes confirmed the Board discussion centred on hiking or holding

Australian retail sales were stronger than anticipated, although there was evidence of some one-off effects

• We expect the RBNZ to remain on hold at next week’s meeting

The meeting should be straightforward, but we don’t think a hike message will be central to the minutes

The minutes from the RBA’s June policy meeting confirmed the Bank discussed the merits of hiking versus no change, with officials opting to leave the cash rate on hold at 4.35%. As expected, the forward guidance was preserved, noting that “it was difficult either to rule in or rule out future changes in the cash rate target”.

Consistent with Governor Bullock’s press conference, the minutes state it’s still possible to achieve the Board’s strategy of returning inflation to target in a reasonable time frame without moving away significantly from full employment, however, challenges have increased and the narrow path is becoming narrower. We retain the view the RBA will remain on hold this year with policy easing likely in 1H25, but acknowledge risks have shifted and views are highly condi tional on the incoming 2Q inflation data (RBA forecast headline: 1%q/q, trimmed mean: 0.8%q/q).

The inflation discussion dominates the minutes with members still noting upside risk to the near-term trajectory, although price pressures are still insufficient to change their assessment that inflation will return to target by 2026. With that in mind, the June policy meeting pre-dates the stronger-than-forecast May CPI indicator, meaning the commentary surrounding inflation and the implied return to target should be treated with caution.

The RBA’s most recent forecast set was released before the FY2025 Budget and, therefore, overlooks the Government’s decision to extend energy/rental subsidies this fiscal year. These changes will be incorporated in August’s SoMP and should have the effect of lowering the Bank’s forecast in FY2025, while lifting the inflation profile from July 2025 as these policies presumably expire.

Lastly, officials appear to be fading the implications from recent upward revisions to the consumption data, noting these data can be interpreted in different ways, which have conflicting implications for the activity and inflation outlooks. For this reason, the minutes note “there were several reasons not to place too much weight on the revisions to consumption”

with a greater-than-normal degree of uncertainty regarding spare capacity and the economic outlook. The read-through is that the RBA remains in data-watching mode and sensitive to the incoming data, with 2Q CPI (31<sup>st</sup> July) the main print of note.

## Retail sales above expectations

Retail sales increased 0.6%m/m sa in May, ahead of both J.P. Morgan (0.2%m/m) and consensus (0.3%m/m) forecasts (Figure 1). The ABS notes that spending was buoyed by the earlier-than-normal end of financial year promotions and sales events, with discretionary retailers offering the largest discounts. The retail data remain difficult to decipher (declining outright in March, steady in April and outperforming in May), but this week’s print doesn’t alter our view that the underlying pace of spending remains fragile, with household consumption likely to average 0.4%q/q sa in 2H24.

Figure 1: Australian retail sales
![](images/c73e6885a3b5970cd6cae3b644c71b8acc6a1102f925d6212e6b20f102d8b7dd.jpg)

With respect to monetary policy, this week’s minutes reserved judgement on the implications of recent revisions to the consumption data, noting uncertainty as to whether the stronger pace of spending in 2023 comes at the detriment of spending in 2024 and beyond. The retail data are an imperfect proxy for household consumption and we caution against reading too much into this week’s print, but we also acknowledge this outcome skews hawkish when viewed in the context of the recent CPI data and the aforementioned GDP/consumption revisions.

Details of the retail report were mixed. Food (+0.7%m/m ), household goods (+1.1%m/m) and clothing (+1.6%m/m) retailers reported the largest gains, while cafes/restaurants (-0.1%m/m) posted a small decline as the sector continues to adjust from one-off spending related boosts in April . Department stores were the notable underperformer, -0.8%m/m.

Week of July 8-12

## Building approvals bouncing back

Australian building approvals (Figure 2) rose 5%m/m sa in May, beating expectations (J.P. Morgan: +2%m/m, consensus: +1.6%m/m). The April results were also revised up from a small fall to a \~2%m/m gain. We noted in the preview that over the last few years, approvals have tended to rebound strongly in May after a weaker April print, suggesting that there may be some residual seasonality in the series.

Figure 2: Australian residential building approvals
![](images/c862b078bd537557aaf35883f120ed1a7c5c56006f9c22ce0e83798f9ad37b9a.jpg)

This week’s figures are consistent with this dynamic, and most of the rise reflects a 16%m/m gain in high density approvals, which tend to be more volatile. Still, detached private sector dwellings, which are a better indicator of trend, rose a solid 2%m/m. Detached private sector dwellings have steadily risen since the mid-2023 lows, and are now back at 4Q22 levels. This week’s print provides further evidence that approvals have troughed this cycle, and should continue to trend higher on the back of demand/supply imbalances and policy support.

## RBNZ remaining put

Next week’s RBNZ decision should be a minimalist affair – the OCR is likely to remain unchanged at 5.50% and this is an MPR meeting, so no MPS/forecast updates will be published. The committee has shown a high pain tolerance and continues to sound hawkish warnings about sticky inflation, although Chief Economist Conway’s recent speech had a more balanced view of core inflation trends. The committee has repeatedly stated that it has considered hikes over the last year, but hasn’t followed through.

There has not been a lot of signal in such hawkish comments (at least with respect to the policy rate) and we have viewed it mostly as a communication device to manage financial conditions. There is no press conference at MPR meetings, and so there are no media follow-ups to deal with regarding specifics of the debate. As such, we don’t expect that the potential hike message will be central to the minutes this time. On the economic commentary, there hasn’t been much top-tier data to refresh the domestic outlook since the last meeting, and the

1Q GDP data landed in line with the staff’s forecast.

However, the partials for 2Q CPI are adding up soft so far, housing is noticeably weaker and the surveys show a loss of momentum in growth into the second half. The RBNZ has also appeared sensitive to the global mood music lately, and so with US inflation having cooled a bit and the CB easing club broadening out, the tone of the global commentary could be a little more dovish.

## Greater China

• China: June manufacturing PMIs point to stable economic performance compared to May

• Hong Kong: Retail sales below expectations again

• Taiwan: Another jump in June PMI reinforces nearterm industrial sector strength

CPI inflation ticked up to 2.4%oya; housing market boom a key focus for the central bank

• Next week: China trade, FX reserves, inflation and credit, Taiwan trade

Following the mixed signals from May activity data, the June NBS PMI report points to continued softness in manufacturing and further fading of strength in service activities. The NBS manufacturing PMI stayed unchanged at 49.5, following the 0.9-pt decline in May (Figure 1). Meanwhile, Caixin manufacturing PMI ticked up to 51.8, beating market expectations. While both Caixin and NBS manufacturing PMI readings in June stayed close to their May readings, the levels are quite different. Caixin manufacturing PMI rose for five consecutive months to the highest level in the past three years (at 51.8), while NBS manufacturing PMI fell below the 50- threshold in the past two months. This is not unusual. The discrepancy between the two sets of PMI readings can be attributable to the differences in sampling methods (Caixin PMI is biased towards export-oriented smaller enterprises) and statistical approaches (different seasonal adjustments), but may also point to uncertainties in the economic outlook at enterprise levels.

Figure 1: China Manufacturing PMIs
![](images/19a4ad063d8ee51f672926f943aa71dc19ecb42223f5bc085050d04e4ef20bec.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, NBS, J.P. Morgan

China’s PMI reading has not been a reliable leading indicator of economic activity in recent years, still June manufacturing PMI readings (both NBS and Caixin) seem to point to relatively stable performance in June vs May. This is supportive of our 2Q growth forecast of a notable softening (from 7.4%q/q saar in 1Q to 3.5%q/q saar in 2Q), but the momentum seems to be stabilizing earlier than the episode one year ago (there was continuous sequential contraction between

April and July in 2023). It is related to earlier policy adjustment this year (in late April politburo meeting) than last year (only until July politburo meeting).

Meanwhile, the contrast between Caixin and NBS PMI readings points at uncertainties in 2H. The uncertainties come from the housing market (the impact of recent housing stabilization measures), consumption (related to weak income expectations and weak consumer confidence especially among upper-middle income families), exports (risks of heightened trade tension) and the pace of policy adjustment in both fiscal (issuance of special local government bond is still significantly lagging behind normal years) and monetary (PBOC’s reluctance for rate cuts and monetary easing) policy.

## Hong Kong: retail sales remain soft

Hong Kong’s retail sales volume came in below expectations again in May, falling 12.9%oya. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that retail sales volume fell 1.4%m/m sa, adding to the 4.1%m/m sa loss in April (Figure 2). Retail sales volume has been on a declining trend for 10 months, as the 3m/3m saar growth rate has remained negative since July 2023. The softness in retail sales is broad-based, as the sequential growth trend (in 3m/3m saar terms) was negative in May except for consumer durable goods.

Figure 2: Hong Kong retail sales volume
![](images/d84948a7f8cad96839a5aa4999015b124b6389e07e6f33223767fed7f9765c45.jpg)

On June 28<sup>th</sup>, China’s Ministry of Commerce announced to raise the duty-free allowance for luggage articles brought into the Mainland by Mainland visitors travelling from HK SAR from the current level of RMB 5,000 to RMB 12,000. This round of duty-free shopping quota does not seem likely to have a meaningful impact on improving HK SAR’s consumption. First, the raised quota still cannot cover most luxury items, yet may benefit items such as cosmetics, electronic gadgets and mid-priced clothing, etc. Second, the price in HK may not be that attractive compared with online platforms in mainland China. Third, as the JPY depreciated, buying from Japan may be more attractive.

## Taiwan: Another jump in June PMI

Following the volatile, moderate upward trend (from a subdued level) since early 2023, Taiwan’s manufacturing PMI readings have picked up momentum significantly since early this year. There were further solid gains in headline manufacturing PMI in June, which rose 2.3-pt to 53.2, adding to the uptick of 0.7-pt in May. The output component rose 2.3-pt to 54.9, adding to the increase of 1.2-pt in May. It hints at further solid momentum in industrial production activity, following the significant upside surprise in the May IP report (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Taiwan manufacturing PMI output and IP Index, sa
![](images/06367e53be6bf6303c6d55ab4aaca200babd988b5620f38debe692a0d8edeb92.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, MOEA, J.P. Morgan

Signals from the manufacturing PMI export orders component have continued to be constructive, with a significant rise of 3.2-pt to 52.4 in June, the highest reading since March 2022, hinting at further upward momentum in external demand. The manufacturing PMI finished goods inventory indicator rose 1.5-pt to 50.4 in June, suggesting manufacturers may be resuming some inventory building, following significant inventory correction in recent quarters. As the rise in new orders outpaced increase in the inventory component, the manufacturing PMI order-to-inventory ratio rose to 1.10 in June (the highest reading since April 2023), adding further constructive signals to the near-term production activity trend.

## CPI inflation ticked up to 2.4%oya

Taiwan’s CPI inflation came in modestly above expectations in June, as headline CPI rose 2.4%oya on a 0.2% m/m sa gain. For 1H as a whole, headline CPI rose 2.3%oya (compared to full-year CPI inflation at 2.5% in 2023). Further details suggest modest easing in food prices, and further gain in energy prices. Core CPI rose 1.8%oya , unchanged from May.

Looking ahead, assuming some moderate indirect effect of the April electricity price hike in the near term, we expect Taiwan’s headline CPI inflation to stay modestly above 2% during July-August, before easing towards 4Q (especially considering higher base effect from late last year). Overall, we expect headline CPI inflation to average at 2.1% in 2024, while core CPI inflation will likely average at 1.9%.

Figure 4: Taiwan headline and core CPI
![](images/7889afe1da8ed0e59ee478763573ef5437bba5e3caf182043f807adfb54765f4.jpg)

On the growth picture, recent dataflow suggests solid growth momentum in Taiwan’s export sector activity and industrial production, led by structural boost for AI-related products and HPC-related demand, with gradual recovery in other tech demand areas likely to come through in the coming quarters. Consistent upward momentum in the external sector seems to be feeding to the domestic front. For the labor market, total employment appears to have picked up lately, while retail sales showed solid sequential growth in May. Amid solid external demand outlook, domestic capex appears to have picked up momentum in recent months. Overall, the risk seems biased to the upside for 2Q GDP (our current forecast at 2.3% q/q saar), as well as our above-consensus full-year GDP growth forecast of 4.3%y/y.

On monetary policy, the Taiwan central bank’s surprising policy rate hike of 12.5bp in March reflected policymakers intension to pre-emptively contain inflation expectations, especially considering the impact of the April electricity price hike. The impact of the electricity price hike on overall inflation pressure seems moderate so far. In addition, the central bank has indicated concerns about some other aspects of the inflation picture, including stickiness in prices for food away from home and rental prices. In this regard, we note that price increase for food away from home (which tends to track core CPI rather closely in recent years) has eased lately, registering a moderate increase at 2.8%oya in May and June, after hovering at above 4%oya pace through last year.

## Korea

• Customs exports recovered in June and 2Q nominal exports recovered from 1Q’s dip

• Manufacturing PMI rose further in June

CPI inflation was soft in June, implying higher odds for an August policy rate cut

The manufacturing sector data this week was upbeat, offsetting the disappointing industrial production data last week. Customs goods exports recovered in June and 2Q’s quarterly export growth rebounded after 1Q’s temporary blip. Manufacturing PMI rose further from May’s elevated level to provide an upbeat signal for manufacturing production going forward. Meanwhile, June CPI inflation came in softer than expected, and our revised annual 2024 inflation forecast (2.3%y/y) suggests downside risk to the BoK’s May forecast (2.6%). Given the more stable inflation data, we think the dovish tone from the MPC should be reinforced in July, increasing the likelihood of a rate cut in August. While we still expect a rate cut in 4Q24 (most likely in October), the decision between an August or October action is now a close call.

## Exports recovered in June with tech

Seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan, customs exports rose 1.9%m/m, sa, back after a 1.0% fall in May, and the threemonth trend growth recovered to 13.3%3m/3m, saar. We expected 2Q’s overall nominal exports growth to recover after a temporary dip at -1.0%3m/3m, saar, in March (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Korea customs exports
![](images/63e9bc86f5883c7f34599bdd34dc9f31b140c13cfe81ac13844df6fad5bf8b67.jpg)

In the product details, tech product shipments led the June exports recovery and the three-month trend for tech exports re-accelerated. Tech product exports rose 6.6%m/m, sa, the third monthly rise, as semiconductor exports rose 5.3% and computer exports rose 9.1%. In three-month trend growth, tech exports rose 43.0%3m/3m, saar, after slowing to 22.5% in May. Non-tech exports edged down 0.3%m/m, sa, following a 3.6% fall in May, and non-tech three-month trend growth rose 2.1%3m/3m, saar in June, continuing to muddle through. Given the upbeat signal from the manufacturing PMI and improvement in tech IP’s detail (rise in shipments among a fall in inventory), we believe that the tech exports’ strong growth impulse should continue to lead overall exports resilience in 2H24.

## PMI inched up further from two-year high

Manufacturing PMI increased by 0.4pts to 52.0 in June, on top of a strong 2.2pt gain in May. The recent monthly gains fully reversed a cumulative 1.8pt drop in February-April, and the headline index posted the highest rise since May 2022. The key sub-indices also remained much higher than their historical averages, as the new orders index rose marginally further to a 28-month high, and the output index ticked down only 0.2pts from a 35-month high in June. The broad-based resilience of the PMI supports our view that IP’s sequential contraction in 1Q was likely a temporary correction from its surge in 2H23 and a modest recovery should follow in 2Q (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP
![](images/7dcb7e7c7171f20afb8ed822313e8bbd52121c3adcd7fb8cf90fb3f56da4b1d4.jpg)

## CPI inflation fell in June

CPI inflation fell to 2.4%oya in June from 2.7% in May, undershooting expectations. Core inflation (excluding energy and food) stood at 2.2%oya for the second month. Seasonally adjusted by J.P.Morgan, CPI fell 0.1%m/m, sa, for the first monthly fall since August 2022, and core CPI rose 0.1%. In the three-month trend, headline CPI’s trend has decelerated to 1.0%3m/3m, saar, from 2.4% in April, and core CPI’s trend gain (2.1%) remained at around the 2% inflation target.

In the details, the June disinflation was led by a fall in fresh farm product prices (-0.9%m/m, sa) and oil product prices (-1.3%). While summer weather conditions are a wildcard for fresh farm product prices near term, the supply side price pressures are likely to have eased. This is because global crude oil prices have stayed below their early April peak, and the government is still cautious about lifting measures designed to mitigate these price pressures. Meanwhile, core prices’ gains were only modest in June, suggesting a stabilizing trend.

Source: NSO and J.P. Morgan

Week of July 8 - 12

Figure 3: Inflation forecast
![](images/49676287f2a0cc7e0282ea2b706836b2e4d1fefbf8655833c5de43b7b98faec8.jpg)
Looking ahead, headline CPI inflation is expected to slow further, potentially falling below 2%oya from the August report (Figure 3), unless there is an unexpected weather-related spike in fresh farm product prices or rise in global oil prices. The July MPC meeting is likely to emphasize a downside risk to the BoK’s May annual CPI forecast (2.6%y/y), as we anticipate the 2024 annual inflation to be 2.3%y/y following the June data.

## ASEAN

Philippines growth to stay below trend with significant sectoral variation within household consumption

Inflation to downshift on rice tariff cut; BSP to deliver 50bp of easing in 4Q24 with risk of earlier move

• CAD to narrow further on exports recovery and weak domestic private investment

Philippines’ 2H24 growth outlook remains soft with significant sectoral variation within household consumption. The recently approved rice import tariff cuts should lead to a material easing in inflation, setting the stage for the BSP to start a rate cut cycle, with the timing of the first move subject to FX performance. We continue to expect gradual improvement in CA/BOP dynamics, driven by a steady expansion in exports and manageable imports pressure as domestic private investment weakens.

## Mind the gaps

Following the sub-par 1Q GDP print (5.4%q/q, saar; 5.7%oya), there are questions surrounding Philippines growth outlook for the rest of the year. The government’s 2024 GDP forecast has been lowered from 6.5-7.5% to 6.0-7.0% in May, while the BSP also stated in its latest Monetary Policy Report that growth will likely undershoot 6% due to the lagged impact of monetary tightening. We maintain the view that this year will bring another sub-trend GDP print (2024F: 5.7% vs. 2010-19 average: 6.3%), although the sequential slowdown in 1H24 is likely behind us.

Household consumption (75% of GDP) holds the key to the overall growth outlook. While solid labor market conditions (i.e., lower unemployment rate, higher real wages) and healthy household balance sheets will continue to underpin consumer spending, there is a high degree of variation, where discretionary/services have outperformed staples/goods in the past year (Figure 1 and 2).

Figure 1: Philippines private consumption components
![](images/bc4a215e5f9b6ece4c68109bea2b66cefb48049f4f5578b6a9ab8782c9a9c636.jpg)

Figure 2: Philippines private consumption components Annualised growth, %
![](images/8867640a8d4fe2cabe1f5e5c727100af4217f85983fecf8190d4b25977aeeb7e.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates 1. Through 1Q24

For some categories (e.g., recreation & culture, restaurants & hotels), the current high pace of growth is likely driven by a “catch-up” in spending, but for others (e.g., F&B, miscellaneous goods & services), the stark difference between prepandemic and current growth rates could reflect the disproportionate effect of high inflation on low-income households, resulting in a so-called K-shaped recovery.

On the capex side, we expect elevated real borrowing costs to continue to weigh on credit demand and loans formation (Figure 3). A key sector to monitor is private construction, which is experiencing some overhang (especially in office and midend condo market), following years of high growth in the prepandemic years, and have been the key source of weakness in domestic fixed asset investment (Figure 4).

Figure 3: Philippines real policy rate vs. credit demand vs. loans
![](images/f6afd1c3cc80fc8f213580a9442fd4103bedbb6fc59b9b8495f0f33b71f3433a.jpg)

Figure 4: Philippines GFCF components
![](images/7bb1c9354f4b41f9bc2708d9f4cd1ac9e4ba693d280098bd34181b2df1b06bcd.jpg)

## BSP set to ease

As expected, the BSP maintained the policy rate at 6.5% in the June MPC meeting. BSP signaled a dovish turn in policy later in the year on expectations that the recently approved rice import tariff cuts (under Executive Order 62) will significantly reduce inflation. The central bank’s risk-adjusted CPI forecast was lowered to 3.1% for 2024 and 2025, down from 3.8% and 3.7% respectively.

We agree that the rice tariff cut is a potential game-changer in the disinflation process. Assuming that retail prices are immediately adjusted lower (by about 20%) with a 70% passthrough rate, we estimate that headline inflation could fall from 3.7% to 2.6% in 2H24. More importantly, the return to the 2-4% target range could be brought forward from 4Q24 to 3Q24 (Figure 5). However, the caveat is that the tariff cuts will be reviewed every four months to gauge their effectiveness.

Figure 5: Philippines headline inflation
![](images/01e50d906d05f1a05192fd83ffe7a9f41c9c67613152c77fbd05764317a2e50b.jpg)
Source: PSA and J.P. Morgan forecast in shaded region

With the rice tariff reduction set to lift the real policy rate further (Figure 6), the BSP have conceded that the odds of an August rate cut have increased. However, the timing of policy easing will also be influenced by external conditions and the peso performance. We maintain our view that the central bank will err on the side of caution and deliver 50bp of rate cuts in 4Q24 (starting October), with the risk skewed towards a move in August.

Figure 6: Philippines real policy rate
![](images/fc8b53e2b1063c25240d6bb1b9869d6e4a6bcc4d320da85ae9b436395baa03b0.jpg)

## Manageable CAD on soft imports

Despite the significant role of business process outsourcing (BPO) services (4.5% of GDP) and remittances (7% of GDP) in the economy, the marginal driver of Philippines’ current account (CA) and BOP is still the goods balance (Figure 7). We tentatively pencil in a gradual narrowing of the CA deficit (US\$2.1 billion) in 2H24 (1H24: US\$3.5 billion), taking the full-year number to US\$5.7 billion or 1.2% of GDP.

Figure 7: Philippines balance of payments
![](images/ed487c1c8cf1dd93ad0d3fc566f14092017f64c8b9c100e9af538f35acbcd8c6.jpg)

A key forecast is for steady expansion in exports, driven by the regional tech upswing and resilient US demand (Figure 8). The import bill should be range-bound with weaker domestic private investment translating into soft capital goods import volume (Figure 9). Our in-house commodity price forecasts suggest the terms-of-trade should also stay manageable (Figure 10).

Figure 8: US durable equipment investment vs. Philippines exports
![](images/d8668ffa9a89b5c5719ed6f87afe3e70df71590b4cbfd214394ccade6e1817f6.jpg)

## India

• Near-term upside risks to inflation emerge from higher food and telecom prices

• June CPI now tracking close to 5%

Pushing out monetary easing till food inflation uncertainty is resolved

This week we push out our forecast for rate cuts from 3Q24 to 4Q24 on the back of a spike in food inflation due to the delayed monsoon. While we expect a normal monsoon and food price normalisation in the coming months, the recent uncertainty pushes out timing of cuts.

## Pre-requisites for RBI rate cuts

Our forecast for monetary easing this fiscal year is predicated on the RBI having conviction that inflation is settling near 4% as growth slows in the coming quarters. To ease, however, several other boxes also need to be checked. Fiscal policy must not deviate far from its consolidation path. Concerns about financial stability – which had generated macroprudential tightening in recent quarters – need to be alleviated. And global financial conditions – manifested most starkly in U.S. dollar movements – need to be conducive to easing in emerging markets. We address each of these in turn.

The first Budget of the new administration later this month will reveal the specific fiscal math, but, as previously laid out, fiscal policy has growing degrees of freedom this year. Central fiscal consolidation has progressed faster than envisaged. The Center reduced its deficit from 6.4% of GDP in FY23 to 5.6% of GDP FY24, more than planned. State deficits widened by 0.2% of GDP, but the larger-than-expected central consolidation meant that the combined deficit had still been reduced by 0.6% of GDP last fiscal to 8.6% of GDP. This year, the Center has committed to reduce its deficit further to 5.1% of GDP. With the RBI’s dividend double what was budgeted in February (0.6% of GDP versus 0.3% of GDP), there is very little likelihood of the Center relaxing its fiscal deficit. If anything, some sections of the market are expecting even greater consolidation.

Financial stability concerns are getting slowly alleviated, as reflected in a slowing of bank lending to NBFCs, a slowing in the growth of unsecured lending (Figure 1), and in a declining ratio of unsecured to total bank lending. May showed some pick-up in loans to NBFC and unsecured lending which would need to be monitored closely. With the RBI keeping a hawk-eye on financial stability, however, we expect these metrics to slow progressively in the coming months. In principle, the RBI could deploy the “two-objectives, two-instruments” approach, with financial stability concerns addressed through tighter macro-prudential measures, and slowing growth being addressed through monetary easing. But, as we have previously discussed, separability is cleaner on paper than in practice, and the RBI would like to see any financial stability concerns largely addressed, before it starts easing monetary policy.

Figure 1: Unsecured Loan Growth by Banks
![](images/2e2ac2ee91cac9bb42c901f4bdbfad4f29d2e286ee9ac0767116ed0a8df70f76.jpg)

On global uncertainty, the Governor made clear that the RBI will not mechanically “follow the Fed” noting that “while we do consider the impact of monetary policy in advanced economies on Indian markets, our actions are primarily determined by domestic growth-inflation conditions and the outlook.” India’s policy rate differential has narrowed from 500 percentage points in 2017 to 100 bps in 2023. It could therefore narrow further given India’s strong Balance of Payments (BoP) position. With markets currently pricing in almost 50 bps of Fed easing in 2024, that should not preclude easing, barring the US dollar strengthening rapidly and putting pressure on the Indian Rupee.

Our forecast of easing is based on growth slowly rolling off. GVA growth slowed from 8.3% in the first quarter of FY24 to 6.3% in the last quarter. We expect GVA growth to remain close to \~6.5% April-June quarter. Last year’s GDP-GVA divergence was on account of much lower subsidies compared to the previous year (-23%). In comparison, this year’s subsidy bill is expected to be much closer to last year (based on the February Budget) and in April-May, major subsidies were just 2% lower. This should induce a much greater convergence between GDP and GVA. For the full-year, therefore, we expect GDP growth closer to 6.5%, lower than the RBI’s forecast of 7.2% of GDP. In recent months, GST collections (Figure 2), auto sales, cement production, formal sector wages (Figure 3), and petroleum consumption have all slowed, suggesting some easing of momentum. That said, top line sales of non-financial firms accelerated last quarter and credit growth and steel consumption are holding up.

Figure 2: GST Revenue (Nominal)
![](images/965ef917762ca687908b76a7ca734cb337ccc94d360a1029e236b7a6813c8c92.jpg)

Figure 3: Staff costs of listed pvt. corporates
![](images/747ec837fc10a3bad5a50f82fc4a2b8f34beeafb73bfe6f9d829b6790db3963f.jpg)

## Delayed monsoon raises vegetable prices

Any easing will be contingent on inflation progressively heading towards, and staying close to, 4%. We have seen progress on that front over the last six months with headline CPI softening from 5.7% in December to 4.7% by May. This process was expected to continue on the back of a strong monsoon with the Met forecasting that, with La Nina expected to replace El Niño, this year would witness plentiful rain, averaging 106% of a normal year. This was expected to depress food inflation – which had averaged 7.7% last year – and in conjunction with core-core inflation at a series-low of 3.2%, keep inflation anchored in the low to mid 4% range in the second half of this year – reflected in the RBI’s own forecast.

Bucking expectations, however, the monsoon has had a slow and uneven start. June rainfall was 11% below normal and even though the deficit has reduced to 3% in early July, the distribution has been less than optimal with most parts of the country facing a deficit even as rains in South India have been much above normal.The delay of the monsoon has meant an extended heat wave in parts of the country and this has pushed up food prices, especially inducing a spike of vegetable prices.

Headline CPI is expected to re-accelerate to 4.9% oya in June, higher than previously envisaged. More generally, inflation in the coming months is likely to be higher than the

RBI’s forecast of 3.8% in the July-September quarter. To be sure, the monsoon disappointment is expected to be temporary. The Met expects July rainfall to be 106% above normal and if this is evenly distributed, and in conjunction with the fact that vegetables are a short-cycle crop, food price inflation should eventually soften. But what events of the last few weeks do is inject some uncertainty into the process, which will keep the RBI cautious in the near term.

## Telecom tariffs to also push up inflation

Additionally, telecom companies have raised tariffs by about \~15% in July. Given the weight in the CPI basket and past translation trends, this should push up headline CPI by another 20 bps or so, starting July. To the extent that this is a oneoff price hike, the RBI should look through this. But again, it adds to the uncertainty of the inflation outlook.

Assuming a strong monsoon for the rest of the season, we still expect inflation below 5% in the second half of the fiscal year, and with growth expected to slow, we still expect monetary easing this fiscal year. But the recent uncertainty pushes out the timing. We are therefore moving our forecast first cut from 3Q (August) to 4Q. For now, we pencil in easing at the December meeting, although we cannot rule out October if economic momentum slows more than expected.

# Asia Focus: China’s industrial capacity utilization

There has been rising concerns about China’s industrial overcapacity. A commonly used measure for the abundance of capacity is the utilization rate. China’s National Bureau of Statistics (NBS) defines it as the ratio of actual output to production capacity, both measured in value. In particular, the production capacity of an enterprise refers to the product output that the enterprise can achieve and maintain in the long term during the reporting period, provided that the supply of labor, raw materials, fuel, transportation, etc. is guaranteed and the production equipment (machinery) maintains normal operation. As such, low rates imply excess capacity. Enterprises with lots of excess capacity tend to lower prices to generate demand, hurting the profitability of the whole sector.

The industrial capacity utilization rate was low at end-2015, at which time the Chinese authorities initiated the previous round of “supply side structural reform”, which focused on reducing distortions in the supply side of the economy and upgrading the industrial sector. The core policy objectives included cutting excess industrial capacity with low profitability and high pollution, de-stocking of property inventories to make room for new capacity, reducing leverage in the corporate sector to lower systemic risk, lowering costs to improve efficiency, and addressing ‘weak links’ in the economy. Indeed, the industrial capacity utilization rate rose significantly in 2016-17, signaling improved efficiency.

China’s overall industrial capacity utilization rate has fallen since 2H21 (Figure 1). Following the peak of 78.4% in 2Q21, it has fallen to 73.6% in 1Q24. The absolute level of capacity utilization rate itself may not necessarily imply excess or insufficient capacity, due to idiosyncrasy across sectors, or differences in measurement, etc. The downtrend may have signaled an imbalance between supply and demand. For reference, the recovery of retail sales lagged behind those of industrial production since the pandemic (Figure 2).

In the breakdown, we observed falling capacity utilization rates in most industrial sectors in 2023 compared with 2017 (Table 1). The capacity utilization rate of the automobile sector fell 7.7%pt to 74.5% in 2023, and fell further to 64.9% in 1Q24. With rising production, the automobile sector has become one of the new growth engines for the world’s second-largest economy. However, the severe competition (esp. in NEVs) has resulted in lower profitability, as the number of loss-making enterprises averaged 29.6% during 2020-2023 vs. 15.7% during 2011-2017. Meanwhile, the capacity utilization rate of non-metallic mineral product (incl. cement, lime, gypsum, brick, glass etc.) in the manufacturing sector fell by

6.2%pt to 64.7% in 2023, coinciding with China’s biggest housing sector correction since 2021.

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
